Long-time dynamics for the Kelvin-Helmholtz equations close to circular vortex sheets
Federico Murgante, Emeric Roulley, Stefano Scrobogna

Abstract
We consider the Kelvin-Helmholtz system describing the evolution of a vortex-sheet near the circular stationary solution. Answering previous numerical conjectures in the 90s physics literature, we prove an almost global existence result for small-amplitude solutions. We first establish the existence of a linear stability threshold for the Weber number, which represents the ratio between the square of the background velocity jump and the surface tension. Then, we prove that for almost all values of the Weber number below this threshold any small solution lives for almost all times, remaining close to the equilibrium. Our analysis reveals a remarkable stabilization phenomenon: the presence of both non-zero background velocity jump and capillarity effects enables to prevent nonlinear instability phenomena, despite the inherently unstable nature of the classical Kelvin-Helmholtz problem. This long-time existence would not be achievable in a setting where capillarity alone provides linear stabilization, without the richer modulation induced by the velocity jump. Our proof exploits the Hamiltonian nature of the equations. Specifically, we employ Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form techniques for quasi-linear systems together with a general approach for paralinearization of non-linear singular integral operators. This approach allows us to control resonances and quasi-resonances at arbitrary order, ensuring the desired long-time stability result.



stix@largesymbols"0E
stix@largesymbols"0F

Keywords:
Kelvin-Helmholtz, vortex sheets, paradifferential calculus,
Birkhoff normal form.

1
Presentation of the problem and main result

The Kelvin-Helmholtz (KH) equations is a classic of fluid dynamics, modeling the intricate behavior of vortex sheets at the interface between fluids with different velocities. Since their introduction by Lord Kelvin and Hermann von Helmholtz in the nineteenth century
[
46
,
47
,
67
,
68
]
, these equations have provided crucial insights into fundamental hydrodynamic phenomena, from the formation of ocean waves to atmospheric turbulence. The classic KH problem addresses the instability of a plane vortex sheet, where the jump in tangential velocity across an interface drives the system linearly, generating the well-known instability that defines Kelvin-Helmholtz phenomena. Of particular interest is the interplay between background velocity jump (
{\mathtt{b}}
) and capillarity effects (
\gamma
), which together determine critical stability thresholds and equilibrium states. This work explores the mathematical structures emerging from these interactions, with special focus on the Weber number
\beta\triangleq{\mathtt{b}}^{2}/\gamma
, which naturally emerges in the equilibrium spectrum and governs the system’s stabilization properties.

We consider a planar Euler system for two irrotational fluids with same density (constant equal to
1
) separated by an interface
\Gamma(t)
homeomorphic to a circle and parametrized by
\mathpzc{z}(t,\cdot):\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}.
This interface divides the plane into two open components
\Omega^{\pm}(t)
with
\Omega^{-}(t)
bounded and
\Omega^{+}(t)
unbounded. Given two functions
f^{\pm}:\Omega^{\pm}\left(t\right)\to\mathbb{R}
we define

\displaystyle\left\llbracket f^{\pm}\right\rrbracket\triangleq f^{-}-f^{+}.

The evolutionary system is thus composed of the following equations

\begin{cases}u_{t}^{\pm}+u^{\pm}\cdot\nabla u^{\pm}+\nabla p^{\pm}=0,&\textnormal{in }\Omega^{\pm}(t),\\
\big(\mathpzc{z}_{t}-u^{\pm}|_{\Gamma(t)}\big)\cdot\mathpzc{z}_{x}=0,&\textnormal{at }\Gamma(t),\\
\left.\left\llbracket p^{\pm}\right\rrbracket\right|_{\Gamma(t)}=\gamma\mathpzc{k}\left(\mathpzc{z}\right),&\textnormal{at }\Gamma(t),\\
u^{+}(t,{\bm{x}})\to 0,&\text{as }|{\bm{x}}|\to+\infty,\\
\nabla\cdot u^{\pm}=0,&\textnormal{in }\Omega^{\pm}(t),\\
\nabla^{\perp}\cdot u^{\pm}=0,&\textnormal{in }\Omega^{\pm}(t).\par\end{cases}

(1.1)

In the above set of equations, the quantities
u^{\pm},p^{\pm}
are respectively the velocity field and pressure inside the domain
\Omega^{\pm}.
The parameter
\gamma\geqslant 0
is the surface tension coefficient and
\mathpzc{k}\left(\mathpzc{z}\right)
is the curvature defined by

\mathpzc{k}\left(\mathpzc{z}\right)\triangleq-\frac{\mathpzc{z}_{x}^{\perp}\cdot\mathpzc{z}_{xx}}{|\mathpzc{z}_{x}|^{3}}\cdot

The last equation in (
1.1
) implies that the vorticity distribution
\bm{\omega}
is localized on the curve
\Gamma(t)
at time
t,
namely

\displaystyle\bm{\omega}(t,{\bm{x}})=\omega(t,x)\delta\big({\bm{x}}-\mathpzc{z}(t,x)\big),

\displaystyle\omega\triangleq\left\llbracket u^{\pm}\right\rrbracket\cdot\mathpzc{z}_{x},

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad x\in\mathbb{T}.

(1.2)

In the case in which

\displaystyle\mathpzc{z}\left(t,x\right)=r(t,x)\ \begin{bmatrix}\cos\left(x+\Omega t\right)\\
\sin\left(x+\Omega t\right)\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\left(t,x,\Omega\right)\in I\times\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle r(t,x)\triangleq\sqrt{1+2\eta\left(t,x\right)},

(1.3)

where
I
is a given interval of time, the system (
1.1
) was recast, in
[
61
]
, as the
Contour Dynamic Equation (CDE)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\eta_{t}=\Omega\eta_{x}-\frac{1}{2}\mathpzc{H}\left(\eta\right)\omega,\\
&\omega_{t}=\Omega\omega_{x}-\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\mathpzc{D}_{0}\left(\eta\right)\omega\right)_{x}-\gamma\left(\mathpzc{K}\left(\eta\right)\right)_{x},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.4)

with

\displaystyle{\mathpzc\displaystyle H}\left(\eta\right)\omega\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\eta_{x}\left(x\right)\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{1+2\eta\left(y\right)}{1+2\eta\left(x\right)}}\cos\left(x-y\right)\right]+\sqrt{1+2\eta\left(x\right)}\sqrt{1+2\eta\left(y\right)}\sin\left(x-y\right)}{1+\eta\left(x\right)+\eta\left(y\right)-\sqrt{1+2\eta\left(x\right)}\sqrt{1+2\eta\left(y\right)}\cos\left(x-y\right)}\ \omega\left(y\right)\textnormal{d}y,

(1.5)

\displaystyle{\mathpzc\displaystyle D}_{0}\left(\eta\right)\omega\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1-\sqrt{\frac{1+2\eta\left(y\right)}{1+2\eta\left(x\right)}}\cos\left(x-y\right)}{1+\eta\left(x\right)+\eta\left(y\right)-\sqrt{1+2\eta\left(x\right)}\sqrt{1+2\eta\left(y\right)}\cos\left(x-y\right)}\ \omega\left(y\right)\textnormal{d}y,

\displaystyle{\mathpzc\displaystyle K}\left(\eta\right)\triangleq\frac{\eta_{xx}-(1+2\eta)-3\left(\frac{\eta_{x}}{\sqrt{1+2\eta}}\right)^{2}}{\left(1+2\eta+\left(\frac{\eta_{x}}{\sqrt{1+2\eta}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\cdot

Throughout the document, we use the notation

\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)\textnormal{d}x\triangleq\frac{1}{2\pi}\textnormal{p.v.}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\textnormal{d}x.

We refer the reader to
[
61
]
for a derivation of (
1.4
) from (
1.1
).
We also warn the reader with the change of notation for the surface tension and mean vorticity with respect to
[
61
]
. Also, here we write the system in a rotating frame with angular velocity
\Omega
but one can easily follow the changes. An explicit computation shows that

\displaystyle\text{for any }{\mathtt{b}}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\left(\eta,\omega\right)=\left(0,{\mathtt{b}}\right)\quad\text{is a solution of \eqref{eq:KH2}}.

(1.6)

Let us define the background velocity jump

{\mathtt{b}}\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\omega\left(x\right)\textnormal{d}x,

which is time independent according to (
1.4
). We can define the invertible change of variables

\displaystyle\psi_{x}\triangleq\omega-{\mathtt{b}},

\displaystyle\psi=\partial_{x}^{-1}\left(\omega-{\mathtt{b}}\right).

(1.7)

The change of variables (
1.7
) allows us to determine the evolution equation for
\psi
modulo a real, time-dependent constant, which is

\psi_{t}=-\frac{\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\mathpzc{D}_{0}\left(\eta\right)\left[\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}\right]-\gamma\mathpzc{K}\left(\eta\right)+\mathpzc{c}\left(t\right).

(1.8)

Since the system (
1.4
) depends only on
\left(\eta,\psi_{x}\right)
, the projection onto the zero-th mode in (
1.8
), which includes the constant
\mathpzc{c}\left(t\right)
, does not influence its dynamics. Therefore, we disregard
\mathpzc{c}\left(t\right)
and we impose that
\psi
belongs to the homogeneous Sobolev space
\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)\triangleq\left.H^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)\middle/\mathbb{R}\right.
, where
H^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)
denotes the classical Sobolev space of periodic real-valued functions, see Section
3
. We can now rewrite the system (
1.4
) in terms of the variables
\left(\eta,\psi\right)
using (
1.8
) and get

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\eta_{t}=\Omega\eta_{x}-\frac{1}{2}\mathpzc{H}\left(\eta\right)\left[\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}\right],\\
&\psi_{t}=\Omega\psi_{x}-\left(\frac{\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\mathpzc{D}_{0}\left(\eta\right)\left[\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}\right]\right)-\gamma\mathpzc{K}\left(\eta\right).\end{aligned}\right.

(1.9)

Notice that

\displaystyle\mathpzc{H}(\eta)[\omega](x)

\displaystyle=2\,\textnormal{BR}(\mathpzc{z})[\omega]\cdot\mathpzc{z}_{x}^{\perp}(x)

(1.10)

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y))^{\perp}\cdot\mathpzc{z}_{x}^{\perp}(x)}{|\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y)|^{2}}\omega(y)\textnormal{d}y

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y))\cdot\mathpzc{z}_{x}(x)}{|\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y)|^{2}}\omega(y)\textnormal{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}\Big[\log\big(|\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y)|^{2}\big)\Big]\omega(y)\textnormal{d}y.

Therefore, the first equation of (
1.9
) preserves the average and the natural phase space for
\eta
is

H^{s}_{0}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)\triangleq\left\{\eta\in H^{s}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{R}})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(x)\,{\rm d}x=0\right\}.

In the absence of capillarity
(\gamma=0)
, it is now understood that the problem is ill-posed in Sobolev regularity
[
30
,
55
,
70
]
, but it admits weak solutions
[
37
]
, while one has to require at least analytic regularity on the initial data in order to have a satisfactory local well-posedness theory
[
65
,
66
,
49
]
. It is also well-established
[
56
, Chap. 9.3]
that the presence of a background velocity jump
({\mathtt{b}})
across the interface drives linear instability in the system, generating the classical Kelvin-Helmholtz phenomena. Conversely, surface tension
(\gamma)
exerts a stabilizing effect at the linear level, enabling local-in-time solutions to the full nonlinear equations
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
34
,
53
]
.
When stabilization is induced solely by capillarity, the existence time is limited to
T\sim\epsilon^{-1}
, where
\epsilon
represents the magnitude of the initial datum. Besides, in the physics literature
[
50
]
the authors perform several numerical simulations for (
1.4
) varying the Weber number
We
which corresponds, up to a period factor of
2\pi
, with the parameter

\beta\triangleq\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{\gamma}

that captures the interplay between the previous two mentioned opposite phenomena. In particular, despite the slightly different geometry considered, it is numerically conjectured in
[
50
]
that the behavior of the system below a critical Weber number, which is very close to (
1.11
), "
…is
quite predictable by linear theory, even over long times…
",
[
50
, p. 1939]
. The aim of our work is to give a rigorous mathematical proof of this numerical conjecture. To do so, we exploit the Hamiltonian nature of the quasi-linear system (
1.9
). Conversely to the classical formulations using the Dirichlet-Neumann operator, here the system is quite explicit and related to singular integral operators, see (
1.5
).
By introducing a novel framework that combines the Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form procedure for quasi-linear Hamiltonian systems
[
27
]
with a generalization of the paralinearization method for singular integral operators developed in
[
19
]
, we prove that the lifespan extends to

T\sim\epsilon^{-\left(N+1\right)}

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
. This result enables us to establish stability way beyond the classical local lifespan results of
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
34
,
53
]
, leading to what we refer to as
almost global well-posedness
. The precise statement is given in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1

(
Almost global existence of nearly circular vortex-sheets
)
.

Let

0<\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<4\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right).

(1.11)

There exists a zero measure set
\mathcal{B}\subset\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]
such that for any values
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
of the surface tension and
{\mathtt{b}}\in\mathbb{R}
of the background velocity jump with

\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{\gamma}\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus\mathcal{B},

for any
N\in\mathbb{N},
there exists
s_{0}>0
such that for any
s\geqslant s_{0}
there exist
\varepsilon_{0},c,C>0,
such that for any
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and any initial datum

\left(\eta_{0},\psi_{0}\right)\in H_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)\times\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right),\qquad\textnormal{with}\qquad\left\|\eta_{0}\right\|_{H_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)}+\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)}\leqslant\varepsilon,

the system (
1.9
) admits a unique classical solution

\left(\eta,\psi\right)\in C^{0}\left(\left[-T_{\varepsilon},T_{\varepsilon}\right];\ H_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)\times\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)\right),\qquad T_{\varepsilon}\geqslant c\varepsilon^{-\left(N+1\right)},

with initial datum
\left(\eta_{0},\psi_{0}\right)
and size

\sup_{t\in[-T_{\varepsilon},T_{\varepsilon}]}\left(\left\|\eta\left(t,\cdot\right)\right\|_{H_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)}+\left\|\psi\left(t,\cdot\right)\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)}\right)\leqslant C\varepsilon.

Remark 1.2
.

Let us make the following remarks about the previous theorem.

1.

The almost-global well-posedness result of
Theorem

1.1

cannot
be achieved in settings where capillarity serves only a stabilizing parameter
, i.e. when
\beta=0
. The Weber number
\beta
, which emerges naturally in the equilibrium spectrum
\left\{\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j)\right\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
(see (
1.12
)), captures the interplay between capillarity stabilization and Kelvin-Helmholtz instability. Remarkably, the incorporation of the (physically relevant) interplay between background velocity jump and capillarity is the key factor that generates stability, in particular we can pass from a Sobolev ill-posed problem (
\gamma=0
) to a almost-global well-posed one when
\gamma
is arbitrarily small and
{\mathtt{b}}^{2}
is comparable. The Weber number
\beta
modulates the linear frequencies
\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j)
in a non-trivial fashion and enables us to exclude resonances

\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j_{1})\pm\ldots\pm\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j_{N})\not=0

taking
\beta
outside a suitable zero-measure resonant set
\mathcal{B}
. The non-resonance condition is a fundamental requirement for implementing the Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form procedure in PDEs
[
27
,
10
,
13
,
9
,
11
,
39
,
12
,
51
,
17
,
16
,
29
,
40
,
18
]
.

2.

Referring again to the work
[
50
]
the authors notice, at page 1939, that

We had hoped to see some repartition of energy from the

k=1
mode to smaller scales over large times. However, for

We=10.0
only a very slow increase is observed, if any, of
the width of the active spatial spectrum.

This unexpected localization phenomenon is consistent with our analysis. Indeed we prove that, after a suitable change of variables, each Fourier mode
u_{j}
can exchange energy only with
u_{-j}
for very long times, as we shall explain later. This is a consequence of the non-resonance conditions and the Hamiltonian structure of the equation which gives the conservation of super-actions (see (
1.19
)).

3.

We identify a threshold for the linear stability in (
1.11
).

This constraint is imposed to ensure that the spectrum of the linearized equation at the trivial state
\left(\eta,\psi\right)=\left(0,0\right)
is purely imaginary (see Section
2.2
), a necessary condition for implementing the Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form argument. While this condition can be relaxed by restricting the phase space to
\mathbf{m}
-fold solutions for sufficiently large
\mathbf{m}
, such a restriction significantly narrows the class of admissible solutions. For more details, we refer the reader to Section
2.2
and
[
61
]
. Although one could adapt the following analysis by verifying the
\mathbf{m}
-fold preserving properties of the transformations along the scheme, following a similar approach to
[
43
]
.

4.

The parameter
\Omega
in (
1.4
) represents the speed of rotation of the reference frame. Since the Kelvin-Helmholtz problem is invariant under rotations,
\Omega
can be chosen arbitrarily without altering the shape of the solutions. We choose
\Omega
as in (
2.22
). This choice simplifies some computations and does not affect generality.

5.

Global-in-time solutions with specific structures can be constructed, as demonstrated in
[
61
]
, where we identified families of globally defined, uniformly rotating solutions. We also refer to
[
69
,
61
,
42
,
62
,
31
,
32
,
59
,
58
,
7
,
8
]
for the construction of families of steady solutions in slightly different settings. However, it remains unclear whether the almost global existence solutions stated in Theorem
1.1
are actually global in time. The quasi-linear structure of the Kelvin-Helmholtz system, combined with the absence of dispersion due to the periodic boundary conditions, makes the global existence of the Cauchy problem currently out of reach.

Ideas of the proof

While the Dirichlet-Neumann operator approach pioneered by Zakharov, Craig, and Sulem in
[
71
,
36
]
has become standard for Water-Waves problems-and also employed to the two phase setting
[
53
]
- we employ an alternative formulation. Following previous works such as
[
35
,
33
]
, we utilize the Birkhoff-Roth integral operator formulation, which exploits the Dirac-
\delta
structure of the vorticity (
1.2
) in conjunction with the Biot-Savart law to express the KH equations as a CDE. A crucial aspect of our approach is establishing that the KH equations thus derived possess a
Hamiltonian
structure (
Section

2.1
), which is the following

\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{t}\\
\psi_{t}\end{bmatrix}=\bm{J}\nabla H(\eta,\psi),\qquad\bm{J}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},

where the Hamiltonian is related to the pseudo kinetic energy
\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}
, the length
\mathpzc{L}
of the free boundary and the angular momentum
\mathpzc{M}
through

H(\eta,\psi)\triangleq\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)+\gamma\mathpzc{L}(\eta)+\Omega\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi).

In different geometrical contexts the Hamiltonian structure of the KH system was already presented by Benjamin-Bridges
[
15
,
14
]
. Once this Hamiltonian formulation is established, we are methodologically committed to working with the specific equations that arise from it, as any deviation would compromise the Hamiltonian property, which is essential for our analysis.
This Hamiltonian structure is a fundamental requirement for obtaining the almost global well-posedness result
Theorem

1.1
using the Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form of
[
27
]
. Since we are looking for a stability result near the trivial state
\left(\eta,\psi\right)=(0,0),
a quantity of interest is the linearization at this stationary solution. The linearized KH system there writes

\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{t}\\
\psi_{t}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{L}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(D\right)\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix},\qquad{\bm{L}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)\triangleq\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-\frac{\left|\xi\right|}{2}\\[5.69054pt]
{\gamma\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2}\left|\xi\right|-\left(\gamma-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\right)}&0\end{array}\right].

The associated spectrum is given by
\lambda_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}^{\pm}(\xi)=\pm{\rm i}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right),
with

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma\left|\xi\right|}{2}}\sqrt{\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}\left|\xi\right|+\beta-1},\qquad\beta\triangleq\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{\gamma}\cdot

(1.12)

Here we see appearing the parameter
\beta
that modulates the equilibrium frequencies. This parameter is homogeneous to a wave number (inverse of a length). The modulation is fundamental for avoiding resonances later in the Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form. Let us mention that the modulation of the linear frequencies by an external or geometrical parameter has been used to avoid resonances and construct quasi-periodic solutions for fluid models, see
[
6
,
22
,
23
,
28
,
24
,
44
,
43
,
45
,
48
,
63
]
. Observe that
\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\xi)
is real for any
|\xi|\geqslant 1
provided that

0<\beta<4\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right).

There emerges our linear stability threshold.This means that one gets linear stability for typically small oscillations at small scales where the stabilizing effects of the surface tension are dominant.
Also notice that the asymptotic of the linear frequencies is superlinear, namely as
\left|\xi\right|\to\infty

\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\xi)\sim\sqrt{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left|\xi\right|^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Our purpose is to prove a nonlinear stability result near the circular interface corresponding to
\left(\eta,\psi\right)=(0,0).
To do so, we are able to obtain a suitable energy estimate of the form, for any
N\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\eta,\psi\right)\left(t\right)\right\|_{s}^{2}\leqslant C\left(s\right)\left(\left\|\left(\eta,\psi\right)\left(0\right)\right\|_{s}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\left(\eta,\psi\right)\left(\tau\right)\right\|_{s_{0}}^{N+1}\left\|\left(\eta,\psi\right)\left(\tau\right)\right\|_{s}^{2}\textnormal{d}\tau\right),

\displaystyle\forall 0<t<T,

(1.13)

where we used the notation

\left\|\left(\eta,\psi\right)\right\|_{s}\triangleq\left\|\eta\right\|_{H_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)}+\left\|\psi\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{R}}\right)}.

Then, a bootstrap argument allows us to get an existence time of the form
T\geqslant c\varepsilon^{-N-1}
where
\varepsilon
is the size of the initial datum. Notice that the above estimate is highly non-trivial for two reasons:

1.

The right-hand side contains the same number of derivatives as the left-hand side, which is particularly delicate given the quasi-linear nature of the equations.

2.

The integral term exhibits high homogeneity, a non-trivial property considering the quadratic nonlinearity of the equations.

To address the first obstacle, we perform a paralinearization of the Kelvin-Helmholtz system (
1.9
) together with a paradifferential reduction procedure to remove the space dependance in the positive order part, which allows to remain at the same level of regularity. The paralinearization result is given in
Theorem

4.2
and writes as follows

\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{t}\\
\psi_{t}\end{bmatrix}={\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{Q}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\eta,\psi;x,\xi\right)+{\bm{B}}_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)\left|\xi\right|-{\rm i}V_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)\textnormal{Id}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ \xi+{\bm{A}}_{\left[0\right]}\left(\eta,\psi;x,\xi\right)\right)}\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix}+{\bm{R}}\left(\eta,\psi\right)\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix},

(1.14)

where
{\OpBWPlain\left(\cdot\right)}
denotes the Bony-Weyl quantization in (
3.9
). Each term of positive order has an explicit formula with

\displaystyle{\bm{Q}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\eta,\psi;x,\xi\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-\frac{\left|\xi\right|}{2}\\
\gamma\left(1+\mathtt{f}\left(\eta;x\right)\right)\left(\left|\xi\right|^{2}-1\right)-\left(\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2}+w_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)\right)\left|\xi\right|+\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{\left(1+2\eta\right)}&0\end{array}\right],

(1.15)

\displaystyle\mathtt{f}\left(\eta;x\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left(\frac{1+2\eta}{\left(1+2\eta\right)^{2}+\eta_{x}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}-1\qquad w_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\left(\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}\right)\frac{1+2\eta}{\left(1+2\eta\right)^{2}+\eta_{x}^{2}}\right)^{2}-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\right),

\displaystyle{\bm{B}}_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)&0\\
B_{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)&-B_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)\end{array}\right],\qquad B_{{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)\triangleq\left(\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}\right)\frac{2\eta_{x}}{\left(1+2\eta\right)^{2}+\eta_{x}^{2}},

\displaystyle V_{\mathtt{b}}\left(\eta,\psi;x\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\mathpzc{D}_{0}\left(\eta\right)\left[{\mathtt{b}}+\psi_{x}\right]-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\cdot

The matrix operator
{\bm{A}}_{[0]}
is of order zero and
{\bm{R}}
is a regularizing matrix operator up to a sufficiently large order. We believe that this paralinearization result is itself of interest for maybe future purposes. In our equations, the Dirichlet-Neumann operator does not explicitly appear, contrary to what occurs in the one-phase flat Water-Waves problem, see
[
36
]
and
[
54
, Chap. 1]
. Instead, the equation derived in (
2.5
) features nonlinearities of convolution type with nonlinear singular convolution kernels. This fundamental difference in structure necessitates the development of a specialized paralinearization technique adapted to these convolution operators—a technique we develop in this work (
Section

4
) building on the previous work of the last author and collaborators in
[
19
]
for the less challenging case of
\alpha
-SQG patches. Let us now expose our method to paralinearize the KH system. The key insight of our approach is that terms that resist standard paralinearization techniques (i.e. paraproducts, Bony paralinearization formula and composition of paradifferential operators) share a common structure of convolution type in the form

H\left(\eta\right)g\left(x\right)\triangleq\textnormal{p.v.}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K\left(\eta;x,z\right)\frac{g\left(x-z\right)}{z}\textnormal{d}z,

where the function
K\left(\eta;x,z\right)
exhibits regularity at
z=0
comparable to
\eta
. By Taylor expanding the function
z\mapsto K\left(\eta;x,z\right)
at
z=0
and applying paraproduct expansions, we derive

\displaystyle H\left(\eta\right)g\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\mathsf{j}=0}^{\mathsf{J}}{\OpBWPlain\left(K_{\mathsf{j}}\left(\eta;x\right)\right)}\ \textnormal{p.v.}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}z^{\mathsf{j}-1}g\left(x-z\right)\textnormal{d}z

(1.16a)

\displaystyle\ +\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}{\OpBWPlain\left(R\left(\eta;x,z\right)\right)}g\left(x-z\right)\textnormal{d}z+\text{ l.o.t.},

(1.16b)

with
J\in\mathbb{N}
, for any
j\in\{0,...,J\},

K_{j}
being a
z
-independant function of
x
and
R
being a remainder satisfying
\left|R\left(\eta:x,z\right)\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(z^{\mathsf{J}+1}\right)
. This transformation reduces our analysis to two specific categories of terms, namely

•

Terms in the right-hand side of (
1.16a
):
Classical theory (see
[
64
, p. 355]
) establishes that for any
\mathsf{j}\in\{0,...,\mathsf{J}\},

\textnormal{p.v.}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}z^{\mathsf{j}-1}g\left(x-z\right)\textnormal{d}z=m_{\mathsf{j}}\left(D\right)g,

where
m_{\mathsf{j}}
represents a Fourier multiplier of order
j
. Thus, through composition theorems for paradifferential operators, we obtain

{\OpBWPlain\left(K_{\mathsf{j}}\left(\eta;x\right)\right)}\ \textnormal{p.v.}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}z^{\mathsf{j}-1}g\left(x-z\right)\textnormal{d}z={\OpBWPlain\left(K_{\mathsf{j}}\left(\eta;x\right)m_{\mathsf{j}}\left(\xi\right)\right)}g+\text{ bounded terms}.

•

Terms in (
1.16b
):
We leverage the decay properties of
R
as
z\to 0
to establish that these terms constitute paradifferential operators of order
-\left(\mathsf{J}+1\right)
modulo smoothing operators, as detailed in
Proposition

3.28
.

The methodology outlined above is elaborated in detail in
Section

4
and formalized in
Theorem

4.2
, representing one of the manuscript’s principal contributions. Our work demonstrates that effective paralinearization is possible even when using the Birkhoff-Roth formulation rather than the Dirichlet-Neumann operator approach. The recovered paradifferential structure in (
1.14
) exhibits similarities with pure-capillarity one-phase Water-Waves equations, which allows us to derive several established results concerning vortex sheets, including the necessity of capillarity for system stabilization (cf.
[
3
]
). Furthermore, we identify purely nonlinear, unstable terms characteristic of the KH equations (cf. the term
w_{\mathtt{b}}
in (
1.15
), which is non-nil even when
{\mathtt{b}}=0
), highlighting the enhanced instability of KH compared to Water-Waves systems. For further analysis of these distinctive unstable terms, we direct the interested reader to
Remark

4.3
.

Once the paralinearization obtained, our goal is to remove the
x
-dependence of the positive order terms in order to run an energy estimate in the same Sobolev space, namely without loss of derivatives. This is done through a classical paradifferential reduction procedure requiring to reformulate the problem with complex variables. Defining the complex coordinates

\displaystyle U=\begin{bmatrix}u\\
\bar{u}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle u\triangleq\mathsf{m}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)^{-1}\eta+{\rm i}\mathsf{m}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)\psi,

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left({\xi}\right)\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{{\left|\xi\right|}}{2\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left({\xi}\right)}},

the paralinearized KH system is equivalent to the complex Hamiltonian system

U_{t}={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\ {\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}_{\frac{3}{2}}\left(U;x\right)\ \omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)+{\bm{A}}_{1}\left(U;x,\xi\right)+{\bm{A}}_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(U;x\right)\ \left|\xi\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}+{\bm{A}}_{\left[0\right]}\left(U;x,\xi\right)\right)}U+{\bm{R}}\left(U\right)U.

(1.17)

In the above system, each
{\bm{A}}_{m}
corresponds a matrix of
x
-dependent symbols of order
m
, while
\bm{R}
is a matrix of regularizing operators up to any fixed order. Then we follow the Hamiltonian method developed by the first author and collaborators
[
27
]
(see also
[
20
,
26
,
41
,
19
,
60
,
57
]
for non Hamiltonian approach) which consists to perform a series of transformations:

i

we first perform an
Alinhac Good Unknown
transformation—a nilpotent matrix-valued paradifferential change of variable introduced in
[
52
,
2
]
. This transformation eliminates the unbounded terms
{\bm{B}}_{\mathtt{b}}
in (
1.15
), which constitute the only unbounded contributions in the one-phase gravity water wave system, thus proving essential for developing local well-posedness theory in the pure gravity setting;

ii

we then
diagonalize
and
reduce to constant coefficients
the resulting system at arbitrary order, modulo smoothing operators (whose regularizing effects depend on the initial data’s regularity). This technique, initially developed in
[
20
]
, has become standard for implementing normal-form techniques in quasi-linear systems
[
60
,
21
,
27
]
. The method involves conjugation with flows generated by paradifferential operators, where generators are selected based on desired cancellations. We reduce the equation to a diagonal, paradifferential constant-coefficient form by iterative application on the degrees of the paradifferential operators.

At the end of such procedure we are able to define a transformed, equivalent (in Sobolev) variable

\displaystyle W\triangleq\bm{B}(U)U,

\displaystyle W=\begin{bmatrix}w\\
\bar{w}\end{bmatrix}\,

that satisfies a
constant-coefficient
,
scalar
equation given by

W_{t}=\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}\bigg(\big(1+\mathpzc{v}(U;t)\big)\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\xi)+\mathpzc{V}_{\mathtt{b}}(U;t)\xi+\mathpzc{b}_{\frac{1}{2}}(U;t)|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathpzc{b}_{0}(U;t,\xi)\bigg)\right)W+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}(U;t)W,

(1.18)

up to a smoothing remainder
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}
(see
Proposition

6.1
and (
3.11
) for the definition of
\OpvecPlain\left(\cdot\right)
). The transformed equation (
1.18
) has the crucial property that its para-differential part is in constant-coefficient form. To overcome the second obstacle
Item

2
, we aim to implement a Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form up to homogeneity degree
N
. However, unlike the original complex system (
1.17
), (
1.18
) no longer possesses the fundamental Hamiltonian structure. This structure is essential to ensure that certain
non-trivial
resonant terms do not contribute to energy estimates, as explained below. Recovering this structure is the purpose of the Darboux symplectic corrector as designed in
[
27
]
. To understand why this correction is needed, we first examine the role of non-resonance conditions. The non-resonance conditions in
Section

2.3
ensure the exclusion of resonances, meaning that

\sigma_{1}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j_{1})+\dots+\sigma_{N}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j_{N})\neq 0

unless the indices
(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{N})\in\{\pm\}^{N}
and
(j_{1},\dots,j_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
are
super-action preserving
(see
Definition

2.5
). This can happen when
N=2p
is even, with

\sigma_{1}=\dots=\sigma_{p}=+,\qquad\sigma_{p+1}=\dots=\sigma_{2p}=-,

and either

\displaystyle\text{(i)}\ j_{\ell}=j_{p+\ell}\qquad\text{or }\qquad\text{(ii)}\ j_{\ell}=-j_{p+\ell}.

Case (i) corresponds to
trivial resonances
. The associated monomials in the vector fields of (
1.18
) take the form

|u_{j_{1}}|^{2}\dots|u_{j_{p-1}}|^{2}u_{j_{p}}e^{{\rm i}j_{p}x}.

Proving that these terms do not contribute to Sobolev energy estimates is typically straightforward, as it suffices to show that their coefficients are purely imaginary. In contrast, case (ii) involves monomials of the form

u_{j_{1}}\overline{u_{-j_{1}}}\dots u_{j_{p-1}}\overline{u_{-j_{p-1}}}u_{j_{p}}e^{-{\rm i}j_{p}x}.

These terms couple different Fourier modes, making it more challenging to show that they do not affect energy estimates. However, if the vector field possesses the strong algebraic property of being Hamiltonian, these monomials—called
super-action preserving
—automatically admit infinitely many conservation laws, known as
super-actions
. Specifically, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the quantities

J_{n}(u)\triangleq|u_{n}|^{2}+|u_{-n}|^{2}

(1.19)

are conserved. As a consequence, a Hamiltonian, super-action preserving vector field remains transparent to any Sobolev energy estimate.
However, the system (
1.18
) for
W

lacks this Hamiltonian structure, preventing direct application of these conservation laws. To overcome this issue, we introduce the Darboux symplectic correction, as detailed in
Proposition

7.1
, restoring the necessary structure and allowing us to exploit these properties effectively. Since the map
{\bm{B}}(U)
satisfies the hypotheses of
[
27
, Theorem 7.1]
, we apply it to obtain a new constant-coefficient equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{0}

\displaystyle={\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)Z_{0}+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0};\xi)+{\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)Z_{0}+\boldsymbol{R}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})Z_{0}+\boldsymbol{R}_{>N}(U;t)U.

(1.20)

This equation is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
, meaning that

\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0};\xi)\right)Z_{0}+\boldsymbol{R}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})Z_{0}=\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H_{\leqslant N}

for some real Hamiltonian function
H_{\leqslant N}
. At this point, we begin the algorithmic procedure of reducing the degrees of homogeneity (see
Proposition

7.9
).
The final outcome is a super-action preserving Hamiltonian equation of the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z

\displaystyle={{\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)}Z+\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}(Z)+\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}(Z)+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)Z+\boldsymbol{R}_{>N}(U;t)U.

(1.21)

Since the super-action preserving Hamiltonian terms
{{\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)}Z
,
\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}(Z)
, and
\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}(Z)
do not contribute to the energy estimate, we obtain, for small solutions
\|Z\|\sim\|U\|_{s}\lesssim\varepsilon
, the energy bound

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\|Z\|_{s}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon^{N+3},

which allows us to prove
Theorem

1.1
.

Structure of the manuscript

The Hamiltonian formulation can be found in
Section

2.1
while the non-resonance conditions are proved in
Section

2.3
.
The paralinearization of the system (
1.9
) is carried out in Section
4
and the final result is stated in
Theorem

4.2
. The next step is to reformulate the results using the complex notation, see Section
5
. Then, we implement a reducibility procedure to get rid of the space dependence of the positive order part. This part is now rather classical and the corresponding final result is given in Proposition
6.1
. With this in hand, one can perform the Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form. It is done in Section
7.2
and requires non-resonance conditions for frequency vectors composed with the equilibrium spectrum. Such conditions are checked in Section
2.3
and are the reasons for the introduction of the zero measure set
\mathcal{B}
.

Aknowledgments :
FM is supported by the ERC STARTING GRANT 2021 ”Hamiltonian Dynamics, Normal Forms and Water Waves” (HamDyWater-Wavesa), Project Number: 101039762. ER is supported by PRIN 2020 ”Hamiltonian and Dispersive PDEs” project number: 2020XB3EFL. SS is supported by PRIN 2022 ”Turbulent effects vs Stability in Equations from Oceanography” (TESEO), project number: 2022HSSYPN.

2
Hamiltonian structure and non-resonance conditions

Here we highlight the Hamiltonian nature of the system (
1.9
). Then, we study the associated linearization at the trivial solution
\left(\eta,\psi\right)=\left(0,0\right)
and discuss the non-resonance property of the corresponding eigenvalues. This latter fact is crucial for implementing the Birkhoff normal form in Section
7
.

2.1
Derivation of the Hamiltonian formulation

Let us now exhibit the Hamiltonian nature of the Kelvin-Helmholtz system (
1.9
).

Proposition 2.1
.

The system (
1.9
) is Hamiltonian. More precisely, let us consider

H(\eta,\psi)\triangleq\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)+\gamma\mathpzc{L}(\eta)+\Omega\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi),

(2.1)

where
\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi),

\mathpzc{L}(\eta)
and
\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)
are the pseudo
kinetic energy, the length of the free boundary and the angular momentum, respectively defined by

\displaystyle\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)

\displaystyle\triangleq-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\psi_{x}(x)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\big(\psi_{x}(y)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\log\big(|\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y)|^{2}\big)\textnormal{d}y\textnormal{d}x,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathpzc{L}(\eta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\mathpzc{z}_{x}(x)|\textnormal{d}x,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{x}(x)\eta(x)\textnormal{d}x.

(2.4)

Then, the equations (
1.9
) are equivalent to

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{t}\\
\psi_{t}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-\nabla_{\psi}H(\eta,\psi)\\
\nabla_{\eta}H(\eta,\psi)\end{bmatrix}={\bm{J}}\ \nabla H\left(\eta,\psi\right),\qquad{\bm{J}}\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.5)

In addition, the Hamiltonian
H
is resversible and invariant under translations, namely defining the transformations

\mathtt{S}\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix}(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
-\psi\end{bmatrix}(-x),\qquad\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix}(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix}(x+\varsigma),

we have

H\circ\mathtt{S}=H=H\circ\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma},\qquad\forall\varsigma\in\mathbb{T}.

(2.6)

Proof.

\blacktriangleright

The pseudo kinetic part:

We compute the variation of
\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)
with respect to
\psi
. Using integration by parts and (
1.10
), we get

\displaystyle\textnormal{d}_{\psi}\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)[\hat{\psi}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\psi_{y}(y)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\partial_{x}\log\big(|\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y)|^{2}\big)\textnormal{d}y\right)\hat{\psi}(x)\textnormal{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathpzc{H}(\eta)[\partial_{x}\psi+{\mathtt{b}}](x)\hat{\psi}(x)\textnormal{d}x

(2.7)

From (
2.7
), we deduce

\nabla_{\psi}\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathpzc{H}(\eta)[\partial_{x}\psi]+\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\mathpzc{H}(\eta)[1].

(2.8)

Now we turn to the differentiation of
\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)
with respect to
\eta
. Recall the notation in (
1.3
).
Differentiating (
2.2
) with respect to
\eta
, we infer

\textnormal{d}_{\eta}\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)[\hat{\eta}]=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\psi_{x}(x)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\big(\psi_{x}(y)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\frac{\hat{\eta}(x)+\hat{\eta}(y)-\big(\hat{\eta}(x)\frac{r(y)}{r(x)}+\hat{\eta}(y)\frac{r(x)}{r(y)}\big)\cos(x-y)}{|\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y)|^{2}}\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}y.

By symmetry, we can reduce this expression to

\displaystyle\textnormal{d}_{\eta}\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)[\hat{\eta}]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\psi_{x}(x)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\psi_{x}(y)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\frac{1-\frac{r(y)}{r(x)}\cos(x-y)}{|\mathpzc{z}(x)-\mathpzc{z}(y)|^{2}}\hat{\eta}(x)\textnormal{d}y\right)\textnormal{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\big(\psi_{x}(x)+{\mathtt{b}}\big)\mathpzc{D}_{0}(\eta)[\partial_{x}\psi+{\mathtt{b}}](x)\hat{\eta}(x)\textnormal{d}x,

which implies in turn

\nabla_{\eta}\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)=-\frac{\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\mathpzc{D}_{0}(\eta)[\partial_{x}\psi+{\mathtt{b}}].

(2.9)

\blacktriangleright

The length part:

Recall that
\mathpzc{z}(x)=\big(1+h(x)\big)e^{{\rm i}x}
with
h=\sqrt{1+2\eta}-1.
Therefore,

\mathpzc{z}_{x}(x)=\Big(h_{x}(x)+{\rm i}\big(1+h(x)\big)\Big)e^{{\rm i}x},\qquad|\mathpzc{z}_{x}(x)|^{2}=h_{x}^{2}(x)+\big(1+h(x)\big)^{2}.

Thus,

\mathpzc{L}(\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sqrt{h_{x}^{2}(x)+\big(1+h(x)\big)^{2}}\textnormal{d}x\triangleq\hskip 3.0pt\widetilde{\hskip-3.0pt\mathpzc{L}}(h).

Differentiating, we obtain

d_{h}\hskip 3.0pt\widetilde{\hskip-3.0pt\mathpzc{L}}(h)[\hat{h}]=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{h_{x}(x)\hat{h}_{x}(x)+\big(1+h(x)\big)\hat{h}(x)}{\sqrt{h_{x}^{2}(x)+\big(1+h(x)\big)^{2}}}\textnormal{d}x.

Integrating by parts, we get

\displaystyle d_{h}\hskip 3.0pt\widetilde{\hskip-3.0pt\mathpzc{L}}(h)[\hat{h}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left[\frac{\big(1+h(x)\big)}{\sqrt{h_{x}^{2}(x)+\big(1+h(x)\big)^{2}}}-\partial_{x}\left(\frac{h_{x}(x)}{\sqrt{h_{x}^{2}(x)+\big(1+h(x)\big)^{2}}}\right)\right]\hat{h}(x)\textnormal{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(1+h(x)\big)\frac{\big(1+h(x)\big)\big(h_{xx}(x)-1-h(x)\big)-2h_{x}^{2}(x)}{\left(h_{x}^{2}(x)+\big(1+h(x)\big)^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\hat{h}(x)\textnormal{d}x.

It is easy to see that

\frac{(1+h)(h_{xx}-1-h)-2h_{x}^{2}}{\big(h_{x}^{2}+(1+h)^{2}\big)^{\frac{3}{2}}}=\frac{\eta_{xx}-(1+2\eta)-3\left(\frac{\eta_{x}}{\sqrt{1+2\eta}}\right)^{2}}{\left(1+2\eta+\left(\frac{\eta_{x}}{\sqrt{1+2\eta}}\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}=\mathpzc{K}(\eta).

As a consequence,

\nabla_{h}\hskip 3.0pt\widetilde{\hskip-3.0pt\mathpzc{L}}(h)=-(1+h)\mathpzc{K}(\eta).

Applying the chain rule, we deduce that

\nabla_{\eta}\mathpzc{L}(\eta)=\nabla_{h}\hskip 3.0pt\widetilde{\hskip-3.0pt\mathpzc{L}}(h)\cdot\nabla_{\eta}h=-(1+h)\mathpzc{K}(\eta)\cdot\frac{1}{1+h}=-\mathpzc{K}(\eta).

(2.10)

\blacktriangleright

The momentum part:

One readily has

d_{\eta}\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)[\hat{\eta}]=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{x}(x)\hat{\eta}(x)dx

and, via integration by parts

d_{\psi}\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)[\hat{\psi}]=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\hat{\psi}_{x}(x)\eta(x)dx=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{x}(x)\hat{\psi}(x)\textnormal{d}x.

Hence,

\nabla_{\eta}\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)=\psi_{x},\qquad\nabla_{\psi}\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)=-\eta_{x}.

(2.11)

Gathering (
2.1
), (
2.8
) and (
2.11
), we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{\psi}H(\eta,\psi)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\psi}\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)+\Omega\nabla_{\psi}\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)

\displaystyle=-\Omega\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\mathpzc{H}(\eta)[\partial_{x}\psi+{\mathtt{b}}].

(2.12)

Putting together (
2.1
), (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) yields

\displaystyle\nabla_{\eta}H(\eta,\psi)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\eta}\mathpzc{E}_{{\mathtt{b}}}(\eta,\psi)+\gamma\nabla_{\eta}\mathpzc{A}(\eta)+\Omega\nabla_{\eta}\mathpzc{M}(\eta,\psi)

\displaystyle=\Omega\psi_{x}-\frac{\psi_{x}+{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\mathpzc{D}_{0}(\eta)[\partial_{x}\psi+{\mathtt{b}}]-\gamma\mathpzc{K}(\eta).

(2.13)

Comparing (
1.9
) with (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) concludes the desired result.

\blacktriangleright

Invariances :
The properties (
2.6
) are easily obtained by changes of variables
x\mapsto-x
and
x\mapsto x+\varsigma
. This ends the proof of Proposition
2.1
.
∎

2.2
Analysis of the linearization of (
1.9
)

Definition 2.2
.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define the space of Fourier multipliers of order
m
,
\tilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{0}
, as the space of smooth functions from
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
to
{\mathbb{C}}
of the form
\xi\mapsto a\left(\xi\right)
such that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}a\left(\xi\right)\right|\leqslant C_{\beta}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{m-\alpha},\

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}},\left|\xi\right|\geqslant 1/2.

Following
[
61
]
, the linearization of (
1.9
) around
\left(\eta,\psi\right)=\left(0,0\right)
is given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\eta_{t}=\left(\Omega-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\right)\eta_{x}-\frac{\left|D\right|}{2}\psi,\\
&\psi_{t}=\left(\gamma\left|D\right|^{2}-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2}\left|D\right|-\left(\gamma-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\right)\right)\ \eta+\left(\Omega-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\right)\psi_{x}.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.14)

Namely, we can write (
2.14
) as

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{t}\\
\psi_{t}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{L}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(D\right)\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle{\bm{L}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)\triangleq\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm i}\left(\Omega-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\right)\xi&-\frac{\left|\xi\right|}{2}\\[5.69054pt]
{\gamma\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2}\left|\xi\right|-\left(\gamma-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\right)}&{\rm i}\left(\Omega-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\right)\xi\end{array}\right].

The eigenvalues of
{\bm{L}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)
are given by

\lambda^{\pm}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)\triangleq{\rm i}\left(\Omega-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\right)\xi\pm\,\sqrt{-\frac{\left|\xi\right|}{2}\left(\gamma\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2}\left|\xi\right|-\left(\gamma-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\right)\right)}\ \in\tilde{\Gamma}^{3/2}_{0}\ .

(2.17)

We want the eigenvalues in (
2.17
) to be purely imaginary, this happens if and only if

\gamma\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2}\left|\xi\right|-\left(\gamma-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\right)>0,

(2.18)

The condition (
2.18
) is satisfied for any
|\xi|\geqslant 1
if

\beta\triangleq\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{\gamma}\in[0,\beta_{+}),\qquad\beta_{+}\triangleq 4(2+\sqrt{3})\approx 14,928...

Indeed, for
|\xi|\in[1,2]
, one has

\gamma\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2}\left|\xi\right|-\left(\gamma-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\right)\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{2},2\gamma\right\}>0

for any
\beta>0
. While, for
|\xi|>2
, it reduces to

\beta<\min_{|\xi|>2}\left(\frac{|\xi|^{2}-1}{\frac{|\xi|}{2}-1}\right)=4(2+\sqrt{3}).

(2.19)

Remark 2.3
.

We consider the restriction in (
2.18
) for
|\xi|\geqslant 1
. If we further restrict to
\xi\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
, we can improve
\beta_{+}
from
4(2+\sqrt{3})
to
\beta_{+}=15
. However, in
Section

2.3
, we extend the function
\lambda^{\pm}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\xi)
to apply the Delort-Szeftel
Theorem

2.7
. We believe that the argument in
Proposition

2.8
could be modified to maintain the slightly less restrictive condition
\beta_{+}=15
. Nevertheless, to preserve the simplicity of our approach, we do not pursue this further analysis.

Then we obtain that

\displaystyle\lambda^{\pm}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)={\rm i}\left(\Omega-\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\right)\xi\pm{\rm i}\ \omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left({\xi}\right),

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left({\xi}\right)\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{\left|\xi\right|}{2}\left(\gamma\left(\left|\xi\right|^{2}-1\right)-{\mathtt{b}}^{2}\left(\frac{\left|\xi\right|}{2}-1\right)\right)}\in\tilde{\Gamma}^{3/2}_{0}\ .

(2.20)

Notice that we can expand
\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left({\xi}\right)
and obtain that

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left({\xi}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\ \left|\xi\right|^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\gamma}}\left(\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\right)^{2}\ \left|\xi\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}};-\frac{1}{2}}\left({\xi}\right),

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}};-\frac{1}{2}}\left({\xi}\right)\in\tilde{\Gamma}^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{0}.

(2.21)

In the sequel, we make the following natural choice for
\Omega

\Omega\triangleq\frac{{\mathtt{b}}}{2}\cdot

(2.22)

Remark 2.4
.

Already at linear level standard computations show that in order to close energy estimates for the system (
2.14
) we need a discrepancy in regularity between
\eta
and
\psi
, namely we can close the energy estimates on (
2.14
) in the case in which
\eta\in H^{s+\frac{1}{4}}_{0}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)
and
\psi\in\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)
, such regularity gap shall persist at nonlinear level as well. This is not unexpected, and the same behavior is present for the one-phase water waves problem, both at linear and nonlinear level, cf.
[
1
]
.

2.3
Non-resonance conditions

In this subsection, we study the non-resonances between the frequencies. This is needed in the application of the normal form algorithm performed in Section
7.2
. Specifically, we prove that there are no resonances between linear frequencies, except for the
super-action-preserving
ones, as defined below.

Definition 2.5

(
{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}
multi-index)
.

A multi-index

(\alpha,\beta)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}

is
super-action preserving
if

\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}=\beta_{n}+\beta_{-n}\ ,\qquad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\,.

(2.23)

A super-action preserving multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)
satisfies
|\alpha|=|\beta|
where

|\alpha|\triangleq\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}\alpha_{j}
.
If a multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}
is not super-action preserving, then the set

\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\beta)\triangleq\Big\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}-\beta_{n}-\beta_{-n}\neq 0\Big\}

(2.24)

is not empty
and, since

\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\beta)\subset\Big\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}+\beta_{n}+\beta_{-n}\neq 0\Big\},

its cardinality satisfies

|\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\beta)|\leqslant|\alpha+\beta|=|\alpha|+|\beta|\,.

(2.25)

The main result of the present section is the following:

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
0<\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<4\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right).
Then, there exist
\tau,\delta>0
and a zero measure set
\mathcal{B}\subset\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]
such that for any
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus\mathcal{B}
the following holds: there is
\nu>0
such that for any multi-index
\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)\in(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\times(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
of length
\left|\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}\right|\leqslant M,
which is not super-action preserving (in the sense of Definition
2.5
), one has

\left|\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\cdot\left(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|\geqslant\frac{\nu}{\left(\displaystyle\max_{j\in\textnormal{supp}\left(\alpha\cup\alpha^{\prime}\right)}|j|\right)^{\tau}},

where

\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\triangleq\left(\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left({j}\right)\right)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}.

The rest of
Section

2.3
is dedicated to the proof of
Proposition

2.6
. This latter is a consequence of the Delort-Szeftel Theorem that we recall here for the convenience of the reader. For its proof, we refer to
[
38
, Theorem 5.1]
.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*},

r_{0}>0
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.
We denote
B_{r_{0}}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
the ball centered at the origin and of radius
r_{0}.
Consider
f:B_{r_{0}}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)\times\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\to\mathbb{R}
a continuous sub-analytic function and
\rho:B_{r_{0}}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)\to\mathbb{R}
a non-zero real-analytic function. We assume the following facts.

1.

The function
f
is real-analytic on
\{x\in B_{r_{0}}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\rho(x)\neq 0\}\times\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right].

2.

For any
\bar{x}\in B(0,r_{0})
with
\rho(\bar{x})\neq 0,
the equation
f(\bar{x},\beta)=0
admit finitely many solutions in
\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right].

Then, there exist
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha_{0},\delta,C>0
such that for any
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
, any integer
N\geqslant N_{0}
and any
x\in B(0,r_{0})
with
\rho(x)\neq 0
, we have

\left|\left\{\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\left|f(x,y)\right|\leqslant\alpha\left|\rho(x)\right|^{N}\right\}\right|\leqslant C\alpha^{\delta}\left|\rho(x)\right|^{N\delta}.

First observe that we can write

\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma\left|\xi\right|}{2}}\sqrt{\left|\xi\right|^{2}-\frac{\beta}{2}\left|\xi\right|+\beta-1},\qquad\beta\triangleq\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{\gamma}\cdot

(2.26)

Notice that
\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(2)=\sqrt{3\gamma}
is independent of
\beta
and that

\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(5)-\sqrt{5}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(3)\equiv 0.

The above relation shows that there no resonance between the modes
3
and
5
. However, the couple
(3,5)
appears to be singular in the analysis of the non-degeneracy for the application of the Delort-Szeftel Theorem. That’s why we need to treat it separately.

Application of
Theorem

2.7

The application of
Theorem

2.7
allows us to control quasi-resonances at arbitrary order via polynomial bounds, thus inducing a finite, but recoverable, loss of derivatives. The result we obtain is the following one:

Proposition 2.8
.

Fix
0<\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<4\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right)
,
\mathtt{A}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Then, there exist
\nu_{0},\tau,\delta>0,
depending on
\mathtt{A}
and
\mathtt{M},
such that for any
\tilde{\nu}\in(0,\nu_{0}),
there exists a set
\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\nu}}\subset\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]
of measure
O(\tilde{\nu}^{\delta})
such that for any
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\nu}},
the following holds: denote
n_{0}=2
,
n_{1}=3
and for any distinct integers
n_{2},...,n_{\mathtt{A}}\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{2,3,5\}
and any
\vec{c}=\left(c_{0},c_{1},...,c_{\mathtt{A}}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathtt{A}+1}\setminus\{0\}
with
\max_{a=0,...,\mathtt{A}}\left|c_{a}\right|\leqslant\mathtt{M},
we have

\left|\sum_{a=0}^{\mathtt{A}}c_{a}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(n_{a})\right|\geqslant\widetilde{\nu}\left(\sum_{a=0}^{\mathtt{A}}n_{a}\right)^{-\tau}.

(2.27)

Proof.

We denote
\vec{n}=\left(n_{0},n_{1},...,n_{\mathtt{A}}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}^{*}\right)^{\mathtt{A}+1}
with
n_{2},...,n_{\mathtt{A}}\not\in\{2,3,5\}
and distinct, we introduce the notations

x_{0}\left(\vec{n}\right)\triangleq\left(\sum_{a=0}^{\mathtt{A}}n_{a}\right)^{-1},\qquad x_{a}\left(\vec{n}\right)\triangleq x_{0}\left(\vec{n}\right)\sqrt{n_{a}-1}.

(2.28)

We note that

0\leqslant|x_{a}\left(\vec{n}\right)|\leqslant 1,\quad\text{for any }a=0,\dots,{\mathtt{A}}.

(2.29)

The condition (
2.27
) is equivalent to

\left|f_{\vec{c}}(x,\beta)\right|\geqslant\frac{\widetilde{\nu}}{\sqrt{\gamma}}x_{0}^{\tau+3},\quad x=\left(x_{0}(\vec{n}),x_{1}(\vec{n}),\dots,x_{\mathtt{A}}(\vec{n})\right)

(2.30)

where

f_{\vec{c}}(x,\beta)\triangleq\sum_{a=1}^{\mathtt{A}}r_{a}\lambda(x_{a},x_{0},\beta)+c_{0}\sqrt{3}x_{0}^{3},\qquad x=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{\mathtt{A}})\in B_{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{{\mathtt{A}}+1}\right),

(2.31)

with

r_{a}\triangleq c_{a}\sqrt{x_{a}^{2}+x_{0}^{2}},\qquad\lambda(y,x_{0},\beta)\triangleq\sqrt{y^{4}+\left(2-\frac{\beta}{2}\right)x_{0}^{2}y^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}x_{0}^{4}}.

The function
f_{\vec{c}}\colon B_{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{{\mathtt{A}}+1}\right)\times\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\to{\mathbb{R}}
is continuous and sub-analytic. Remark that for any
l\in\mathbb{N}^{*},

\partial_{\beta}^{l}\lambda(y,x_{0},\beta)=\binom{\frac{1}{2}}{l}\mu^{l}(y,x_{0},\beta)\lambda(y,x_{0},\beta),\qquad\mu(y,x_{0},\beta)\triangleq\frac{x_{0}^{2}\left(x_{0}^{2}-y^{2}\right)}{x_{0}^{2}\left(x_{0}^{2}-y^{2}\right)\beta+2y^{2}\left(y^{2}+2x_{0}^{2}\right)}\cdot

(2.32)

Note that, since
0<{\beta}<\beta_{2}<4(2+\sqrt{3})
,
the denominator in (
2.32
) satisfies

x_{0}^{2}\left(x_{0}^{2}-y^{2}\right)\beta+2y^{2}\left(y^{2}+2x_{0}^{2}\right)>0\quad\text{for any }y\in[0,1],\ x_{0}\in(0,1].

(2.33)

We introduce the polynomial function
\rho:[-1,1]^{\mathtt{A}+1}\to\mathbb{R}

\rho(x_{0},x_{1},...,x_{\mathtt{A}})\triangleq x_{0}\prod_{a=1}^{\mathtt{A}}\left(x_{a}^{2}-x_{0}^{2}\right)\prod_{1\leqslant a<b\leqslant\mathtt{A}}\left(\left(x_{0}^{2}-x_{a}^{2}\right)x_{b}^{2}\left(x_{b}^{2}+2x_{0}^{2}\right)-\left(x_{0}^{2}-x_{b}^{2}\right)x_{a}^{2}\left(x_{a}^{2}+2x_{0}^{2}\right)\right).

The condition (
2.33
) implies that the function
f_{\vec{c}}
is real-analytic on
\{\rho\not=0\}\times\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]
.
We fix now
\bar{x}=\left(\bar{x}_{0},\dots\bar{x}_{\mathtt{A}}\right)
such that
\rho(\bar{x})\not=0
. Then
\bar{x}_{0}\not=0
and, in view also of (
2.33
), one has

\mu\left(\bar{x}_{a},\bar{x}_{0},{\beta}\right)\not=\mu\left(\bar{x}_{b},\bar{x}_{0},{\beta}\right),\quad\text{for any }\beta\in\left(0,4(2+\sqrt{3})\right).

(2.34)

The following Lemma ensures that the function
f_{\vec{c}}(x,\beta)
fullfills the second assumption of Delort-Szeftel Theorem
2.7
.

Lemma 2.9
.

The solutions of the equation

f_{\vec{c}}\left(\bar{x},\beta\right)=0,

if any, are finitely many.

Proof.

Since the function
\beta\to f_{\vec{c}}\left(\bar{x},\beta\right)
is analytic for
\beta\in[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]
, we are only left to prove that it is not identically zero. To do so we fix
\bar{\beta}\in(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
and we note that

\forall l=1,...,\mathtt{A},\quad\partial_{\beta}^{l}f\left(\bar{x},\bar{\beta}\right)=0\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad A\left(\bar{x}\right)\vec{r}=0,

where
\vec{r}\triangleq\left(r_{1},...,r_{\mathtt{A}}\right)
and

A\left(\bar{x}\right)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\mu\left(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\lambda\left(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)&...&\mu\left(\bar{x}_{\mathtt{A}},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\lambda\left(\bar{x}_{\mathtt{A}},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\\
\mu^{2}\left(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\lambda\left(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)&...&\mu^{2}\left(\bar{x}_{\mathtt{A}},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\lambda\left(\bar{x}_{\mathtt{A}},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\mu^{\mathtt{A}}\left(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\lambda\left(\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)&...&\mu^{\mathtt{A}}\left(\bar{x}_{\mathtt{A}},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\lambda\left(\bar{x}_{\mathtt{A}},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\vec{c}\not=0
and
\rho(\bar{x})\not=0
we have also
\bar{x}_{0}\not=0
and
\vec{r}\neq 0.
Then
\beta\mapsto f(\bar{x},\beta)\equiv 0
implies that
\det\left(A(\bar{x})\right)=0.
Besides, by
\mathtt{A}
-linearity of the determinant and recognizing a Vandermonde determinant, we find

\det\left(A\left(\bar{x}\right)\right)=\prod_{a=1}^{\mathtt{A}}\mu\left(\bar{x}_{a},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\lambda\left(\bar{x}_{a},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\prod_{1\leqslant a<b\leqslant\mathtt{A}}\left(\mu\left(\bar{x}_{a},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)-\mu\left(\bar{x}_{b},\bar{x}_{0},\bar{\beta}\right)\right).

By construction, since
\bar{x}\in\{\rho\neq 0\}
, one has that
\det\left(A\left(\bar{x}\right)\right)\neq 0
at the given
\overline{\beta}.
So
\beta\mapsto f(\bar{x},\beta)
cannot be identically zero proving Lemma
2.9
.
∎

We thus conclude that
there are
N_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
,
\alpha_{0},\delta,C>0
, such that for any
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{0}]
, any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
,
N\geqslant N_{0}
, any
x\in X
with
\rho(x)\neq 0
,

\left|\left\{\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\left|f_{\vec{c}}\,(x,\beta)\right|\leqslant\alpha\left|\rho(x)\right|^{N}\right\}\right|\leqslant C\alpha^{\delta}\,\left|\rho(x)\right|^{N\delta}\,.

(2.35)

To conclude the proof we need the following:

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\tau_{1}\triangleq 2\mathtt{A}+1+8\binom{\mathtt{A}}{2}.
Then

\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\mathtt{A}}n_{a}\right)^{-\tau_{1}}\lesssim\left|\rho\left(x(\vec{n})\right)\right|\lesssim\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\mathtt{A}}n_{a}\right)^{-1}.

(2.36)

Proof.

By definition (
2.28
), we have

\displaystyle\left((x_{0}^{2}-x_{a}^{2})x_{b}^{2}(x_{b}^{2}+2x_{0}^{2})-(x_{0}^{2}-x_{b}^{2})x_{a}^{2}(x_{a}^{2}+2x_{0}^{2})\right)_{|x_{0}=x_{0}\left(\vec{n}\right),\,x_{a}=x_{a}\left(\vec{n}\right),\,x_{b}=x_{b}\left(\vec{n}\right)}

\displaystyle=x_{0}^{8}\left(\vec{n}\right)\left[\left(2-n_{a}\right)\left(n_{b}^{2}-1\right)-\left(2-n_{b}\right)\left(n_{a}^{2}-1\right)\right].

The equation

\left(2-n_{a}\right)\left(n_{b}^{2}-1\right)=\left(2-n_{b}\right)\left(n_{a}^{2}-1\right)

is equivalent to

\left(2-n_{a}\right)\left(n_{b}-n_{a}\right)\left(n_{b}-\frac{2n_{a}-1}{n_{a}-2}\right)=0.

(2.37)

Since
n_{a}\neq 2
and
n_{b}\neq n_{a},
we must have
n_{b}=\frac{2n_{a}-1}{n_{a}-2}\cdot

But

2n_{a}-1=2(n_{a}-2)+3.

Hence

2n_{a}-1\equiv 3\quad\text{mod.}\ [n_{a}-2].

Therefore

n_{a}-2|2n_{a}-1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad n_{a}\in\{3,5\}.

We conclude that the only non-trivial couples of integers solutions to (
2.37
) are

(3,5)\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad(5,3).

However, we have excluded the possibility
n_{a}=5
or
n_{b}=5.
This implies that the equation (
2.37
) is not solved with our choice of
\vec{n}
. Thus,

\left|\left(\left(x_{0}^{2}-x_{a}^{2}\right)x_{b}^{2}\left(x_{b}^{2}+2x_{0}^{2}\right)-\left(x_{0}^{2}-x_{b}^{2}\right)x_{a}^{2}\left(x_{a}^{2}+2x_{0}^{2}\right)\right)_{|x_{0}=x_{0}\left(\vec{n}\right),\,x_{a}=x_{a}\left(\vec{n}\right),\,x_{b}=x_{b}\left(\vec{n}\right)}\right|\geqslant x_{0}^{8}(\vec{n}).

(2.38)

Moreover, since
n_{a}\not=2
for
a=1,\ldots,\mathtt{A}
, one has

\left|x_{a}^{2}\left(\vec{n}\right)-x_{0}^{2}\left(\vec{n}\right)\right|\geqslant x_{0}^{2}\left(\vec{n}\right).

(2.39)

Gathering
Equations

2.38
and
2.39
we obtain the lower bound in (
2.36
). To prove the upper bound it is sufficient to use the bounds in (
2.29
).
∎

Consider the set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha,N)

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{n}=(n_{2},\ldots,n_{\mathtt{A}})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathtt{A}}},\,1\leqslant n_{1}<\ldots<n_{{\mathtt{A}}}\\
\vec{c}\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{*})^{{\mathtt{A}}},\,|\vec{c}|_{\infty}\leqslant\mathtt{M}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{B}}_{\vec{c},\vec{n}}(\alpha,N)\subset\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right],

(2.40)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{\vec{c},\vec{n}}(\alpha,N)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad\left|f_{\vec{c}}\left(x(\vec{n}),\beta\right)\right|\leqslant\alpha\left|\rho\left(x(\vec{n})\right)\right|^{N}\right\}.

We fix now
\underline{N}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\bar{N}\delta>1
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}\triangleq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha,\underline{N})
. Then we get

\big|{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}\big|\leqslant C({\mathtt{A}},\mathtt{M})\alpha^{\delta}\sum_{n_{2},\ldots,n_{\mathtt{A}}\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left(\sum_{a=0}^{\mathtt{A}}n_{a}\right)^{-N\delta}\leqslant C^{\prime}({\mathtt{A}},\mathtt{M})\alpha^{\delta}

(2.41)

In conclusion,
for any
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}
, for any

\vec{n}=(n_{0},n_{1},\ldots,n_{\mathtt{A}})
as in the hypothesis,
any

\vec{c}\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{*})^{{\mathtt{A}}}
with
|\vec{c}\,|_{\infty}\leqslant{\mathtt{M}}
,
it results,
by (
2.40
) and (
2.36
), that

\left|{f_{\vec{c}}\,\left(x(\vec{n}),\beta\right)}\right|>\alpha\left|\rho\left(x(\vec{n})\right)\right|^{\underline{N}}\geqslant c({\mathtt{A}})\alpha\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\mathtt{A}}n_{a}\right)^{-\tau_{1}\underline{N}}.

(2.42)

Recalling the definition of

f_{\vec{c}}
in (
2.31
) and
x_{0}(\vec{n})
in (
2.28
),
the lower bound (
2.42
)
implies (
2.8
) with
\tau\triangleq\tau_{1}\underline{N}-3
(cfr. (
2.30
))
and re-denoting
\tilde{\nu}\triangleq\alpha c({\mathtt{A}})
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.6

Fix a multi-index
\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)\in(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\times(\mathbb{N}^{*})^{\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
of length
\left|\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}\right|\leqslant M.
We denote
\mathfrak{N}\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)
as in (
2.24
).
Since the couple
(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is not super-action preserving, then

\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\neq\varnothing.

(2.43)

Then, we can write

\displaystyle\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\cdot\left(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\left|j\right|\right)\left(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\right)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(n)\left(\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}-\alpha_{n}^{\prime}-\alpha_{-n}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathfrak{N}\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(n)\left(\alpha_{n}+\alpha_{-n}-\alpha_{n}^{\prime}-\alpha_{-n}^{\prime}\right).

We use the notation

\mathtt{A}\triangleq\left|\mathfrak{N}\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)\setminus\{2,3,5\}\right|+1.

We also denote

\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\setminus\{2,3,5\}=\{n_{2},...n_{\mathtt{A}}\}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\cdot\left(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\right)=\sum_{a=2}^{\mathtt{A}}c_{a}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(n_{a})+c_{0}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(2)+c_{1}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(3),

with

c_{0}\triangleq\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{-2}-\alpha_{2}^{\prime}-\alpha_{-2}^{\prime},\qquad c_{1}\triangleq\left(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{-3}-\alpha_{3}^{\prime}-\alpha_{-3}^{\prime}\right)+\sqrt{5}\left(\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{-5}-\alpha_{5}^{\prime}-\alpha_{-5}^{\prime}\right)

and for any
a=2,...,\mathtt{A},

c_{a}\triangleq\alpha_{n_{a}}+\alpha_{-n_{a}}-\alpha_{n_{a}}^{\prime}-\alpha_{-n_{a}}^{\prime}.

Observe that for any
a=0,...,\mathtt{A},

\left|c_{a}\right|\leqslant 4M
, where
M
is defined in
Proposition

2.6
. Let us assume the absurd hypothesis
\vec{c}\triangleq(c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},...,c_{\mathtt{A}})=0.
By definition, under such absurd hypothesis, we obtain that
\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\subset\{3,5\}.
But
c_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{5}]
so
c_{1}=0
implies also that
3,5\not\in\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}).
Hence,
\mathfrak{N}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=\varnothing
which is a contradiction with (
2.43
), thus we can safely assume
\vec{c}\neq 0
. We consider the set

\mathcal{B}\triangleq\bigcap_{\tilde{\nu}\in(0,\nu_{0})}\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\nu}},

where
\nu_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\nu}}
are introduced in Proposition
2.8
.
Hence, for any
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus{\mathcal{B}}
there is
\tilde{\nu}\in(0,\nu_{0})
such that
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus{\mathcal{B}}_{\tilde{\nu}}
and, applying Proposition
2.8
we get the desired result with
\nu\triangleq\frac{\tilde{\nu}}{M^{\tau}}
.∎

3
Functional setting

Throughout the document, we shall use the notations

\mathbb{N}\triangleq\{0,1,2,...\},\qquad\mathbb{N}^{*}\triangleq\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\},\qquad\mathbb{Z}\triangleq\mathbb{N}\cup(-\mathbb{N}),\qquad{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}\triangleq{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}.

Along the paper we deal with real parameters

s\geqslant s_{0}\gg K\gg\varrho\gg N\geqslant 0,

(3.1)

where
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The values of
s,s_{0},K
and
\varrho
may vary from line to line while still being true the relation (
3.1
).
We expand a
2\pi
-periodic function
u\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}})
in Fourier series as

\displaystyle u(x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{u}\left(j\right)e^{{\rm i}jx},

\displaystyle\hat{u}\left(j\right)\triangleq{\cal F}_{x\to j}\left(j\right)\triangleq u_{j}\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)e^{-{\rm i}jx}\,{\rm d}x.

The function
u
is real-valued if and only if
\overline{u_{j}}=u_{-j}
, for any
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For any
s\in\mathbb{R},
we define the Sobolev space
H^{s}\triangleq H^{s}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}})

with norm

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{s}\triangleq\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s}}=\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle j\right\rangle^{2s}\left|\hat{u}\left(j\right)\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\left\langle j\right\rangle\triangleq\max\left\{1,\left|j\right|\right\}.

We define
\Pi_{0}u\triangleq u_{0}
the average of
u
and

\Pi_{0}^{\bot}\triangleq\textnormal{Id}-\Pi_{0}.

We define
H^{s}_{0}
the subspace of zero average functions of
H^{s}
for which
we also denote
\left\|u\right\|_{s}=\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s}}=\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s}_{0}}
, and with
\dot{H}^{s}\triangleq\left.H^{s}\middle/\mathbb{C}\right.
endowed with the norm
\left\|u\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\triangleq\left\|\Pi_{0}^{\bot}u\right\|_{s}
.
We define, on
\dot{L}^{2}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}})\triangleq\dot{H}^{0}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}})
,
the complex scalar product
\left\langle\cdot\ \middle|\ \cdot\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
and the real symmetric bilinear form
\left\langle\cdot\ \middle|\ \cdot\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}
as follows: for any
u,v\in\dot{L}^{2}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}})
,

\displaystyle\left\langle u\ \middle|\ v\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}}\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Pi_{0}^{\bot}u(x)\,\overline{\Pi_{0}^{\bot}{v(x)}}\,{\rm d}x,

\displaystyle\left\langle u\ \middle|\ v\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}}}\triangleq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Pi_{0}^{\bot}u(x)\,\Pi_{0}^{\bot}{v(x)}\,{\rm d}x\,.

(3.2)

Moreover we define the real subspace of
\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2})

\dot{H}^{s}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\triangleq\left\{U=\begin{bmatrix}u^{+}\\
u^{-}\end{bmatrix}\in\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad u^{-}=\overline{u^{+}}\right\}.

We also denote

\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2})\triangleq\bigcap_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}),\qquad\dot{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2})\triangleq\bigcap_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}\dot{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}).

Given an interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
symmetric with respect to
t=0

and
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define the space

C_{*}^{K}\left(I;\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\right)\triangleq\bigcap_{k=0}^{K}C^{k}\left(I;\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}k}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\right),

endowed with the norm

\sup_{t\in I}\left\|U(t,\cdot)\right\|_{K,s}\qquad{\rm where}\qquad\left\|U(t,\cdot)\right\|_{K,s}\triangleq\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left\|\partial_{t}^{k}U(t,\cdot)\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{3}{2}k}}.

(3.3)

We also consider its subspace

C_{*{\mathbb{R}}}^{K}\left(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\right)\triangleq\left\{U\in C_{*}^{K}\left(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\right)\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad U=\begin{pmatrix}u\\
\bar{u}\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Given
r>0
we set
B^{K}_{s}(I;r)
the ball of radius
r
in
C_{*}^{K}\left(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\right)
and by

B^{K}_{s,{\mathbb{R}}}(I;r)
the ball of radius
r
in
C_{*{\mathbb{R}}}^{K}\left(I,\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)\right)
.

A vector field
X(u)
is
translation invariant
if

X\circ\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}=\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}\circ X,\qquad\forall\varsigma\in{\mathbb{R}},

where the translation operator
\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}
is defined by

\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}\colon u(x)\mapsto u(x+\varsigma).

Given a linear operator
R(u)[\cdot]
acting on
L^{2}_{0}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}})\triangleq H^{0}_{0}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}})

we associate the linear operator defined by the relation

\overline{R(u)}v\triangleq\overline{R(u)\overline{v}}
for any
v\in L^{2}_{0}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}).

An operator
R(u)
is
real
if
R(u)=\overline{R(u)}
for any
u
real.

3.1
Paradifferential calculus

We introduce the para-differential calculus developed in
[
20
,
27
]
.

Classes of symbols.

Roughly speaking the class
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{m}
contains symbols of order
m
and homogeneity
p
in
u
, whereas the class
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}
contains non-homogeneous symbols of order
m
that vanish at degree at least
p
in
u
and that are
(K-K^{\prime})
-times differentiable in
t
. We can think the parameter
K^{\prime}
like the number of time derivatives of
u
that are contained in the symbols.

Definition 3.1

(Symbols)
.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
p,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K^{\prime}\leqslant K
, and
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

i)

p
-Homogeneous symbols.
We denote by
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
the space of
p
-linear symmetric maps from
\left(\dot{H}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}\right)\right)^{p}
to

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times{\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{C}})
,

(x,\xi)\mapsto a_{p}(U_{1},\dots,U_{p};x,\xi)
whose associated polynomial has the form

a_{p}(U;x,\xi)\triangleq a_{p}(U,\dots,U;x,\xi)\triangleq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\jmath}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{p}\\
\vec{\sigma}\in\{\pm\}^{p}\end{subarray}}a_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}(\xi)u_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}\,e^{{\rm i}(\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{\jmath})x},

(3.4)

where
a_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}(\xi)
are complex valued Fourier multipliers, satisfying

a_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}(\xi)\triangleq a_{j_{1},\dots,j_{p}}^{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{p}}(\xi)=a_{j_{\pi(1)},\dots,j_{\pi(p)}}^{\sigma_{\pi(1)},\dots,\sigma_{\pi(p)}}(\xi)\quad\text{ for any }\pi\text{ permutation of }\{1,\dots,p\}\,,

and
for some
\mu\geqslant 0
,

|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}(\xi)|\leqslant C_{\beta}\left\langle\vec{\jmath}\right\rangle^{\mu}\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta},\quad\forall\vec{\jmath}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{p},\,\vec{\sigma}\in\{\pm\}^{p},\,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(3.5)

We have used the following notations for given
\vec{\jmath}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{p})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{p}
and
\vec{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{p})\in\{\pm\}^{p},

\langle\vec{\jmath}\rangle\triangleq\max(1,|\vec{\jmath}|),\qquad|\vec{\jmath}|\triangleq\max(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p}|),\qquad u_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}\triangleq u_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}\dots u_{j_{p}}^{\sigma_{p}},

where for a fixed
u\in\mathbb{C},
we denote

u^{+}\triangleq u\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad u^{-}\triangleq\overline{u}.

We denote by
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{0}
the space of constant coefficients symbols
\xi\mapsto a(\xi)
which satisfy (
3.5
) with
\mu=0
.

ii)

Non-homogeneous symbols.
We denote by
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[\epsilon_{0}]
the space of functions
a(U;t,x,\xi)
,
defined for
U\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}\right)
for some
s_{0}
large enough, with complex values, such that for any
0\leqslant k\leqslant K-K^{\prime}
, any
s\geqslant s_{0}
, there is
0<\epsilon_{0}(s)<\epsilon_{0}
such that for any
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}
the following holds. There is
C\triangleq C_{s,\beta}>0
such that for any
U\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}(s)\right)\cap C_{*}^{k+K^{\prime}}\left(I;\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)\right)
and
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
, with
\alpha\leqslant s-s_{0}
one has the estimate

\left|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a\left(U;t,x,\xi\right)\right|\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}\|U\|_{k+K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{p-1}\|U\|_{k+K^{\prime},s}\,.

(3.6)

If
p=0
the right hand side has to be replaced by
C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}
.

We say that a non-homogeneous symbol
a(U;x,\xi)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

U\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0},{\mathbb{R}}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}\right)
.

iii)

Symbols.
We denote by
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[\epsilon_{0},N]
the space of
symbols

a(U;t,x,\xi)=\sum_{q=p}^{N}a_{q}\left(U;x,\xi\right)+a_{>N}(U;t,x,\xi)

(3.7)

where
a_{q}
,
q=p,\dots,N

are homogeneous symbols in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{q}^{m}
and

a_{>N}
is
a non-homogeneous symbol in
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}^{m}
.

We say that a symbol
a(U;t,x,\xi)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

U\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0},{\mathbb{R}}}(I;\epsilon_{0})
.

We shall also denote with
\Sigma_{p}^{N}\Gamma^{m}_{q}
the subspace of
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[\epsilon_{0},N]
made of pluri-homogeneous symbols, namely symbols which expand as in (
3.7
) with
a_{>N}\equiv 0
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let us make the following remarks.

•

Given a
p
-homogeneous symbol
a_{p}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
, we shall often, with a slight abuse of notation, identify the associated
p
-homogeneous polynomial with the
p
-linear symbol itself

a_{p}(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p};x,\xi)\quad\leftrightsquigarrow\quad a_{p}(U;x,\xi)\triangleq a_{p}(U,\ldots,U;x,\xi).

This identification is harmless due to the standard correspondence between
p
-homogeneous polynomials and symmetric
p
-linear maps.

•

If
a(U;\cdot)
is a homogeneous
symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{m}
then it belongs to
the class of non-homogeneous symbols

\Gamma^{m}_{K,0,p}[\epsilon_{0}]
, for any
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

•

The classical properties expected for a symbol hold:
if
a
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]

then
\partial_{x}a
is in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
and

\partial_{\xi}a
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{m-1}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.
If in addition
b
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
then
their product

ab
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m+m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p+p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.

We also define classes of functions in analogy with our classes of symbols.

Definition 3.3

(Functions)
.

Let
p,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K^{\prime}\leqslant K
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
We denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
, resp.
\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
,

\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
the subspace of
\tilde{\Gamma}^{0}_{p}
, resp.
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
,
resp.
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
made of those symbols which are independent of
\xi
.
We write
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{{\mathbb{R}}}_{p}
, resp.
\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{{\mathbb{R}}}[\epsilon_{0}]
,

\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{{\mathbb{R}}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
, to denote functions in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
,
resp.
\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
,
\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
which are real valued for any
u\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}\right)
.
