, lemma 6.9]
for details.
∎

Finally we prove
Proposition

6.1
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.1

In
Lemma

6.7
we set

n_{2}\triangleq n_{2}\left(\varrho,N\right)\triangleq n\geqslant 2\left(\varrho+3\left(N+1\right)\right),\qquad\underline{K^{\prime}}\triangleq K^{\prime\prime}+1

and we define

W\triangleq Z_{n_{2}}={\bm{B}}\left(U;t\right)U,

where

{\bm{B}}\left(U;t\right)\triangleq{\bm{\Theta}}_{n_{2}}\left(U;t\right)\circ{\bm{\Phi}}_{n_{1}}\left(U;t\right)\circ{\bm{\Psi}}_{2}\left(U;t\right)\circ{\bm{\Psi}}_{1}\left(U;t\right)\circ{\bm{G}}\left(U;t\right).

At last we set
\mathpzc{b}_{0}^{\left(n\right)}\triangleq\mathpzc{b}_{0}
and we conclude. ∎

7
Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form and energy estimate

In this section we finally prove the almost global existence Theorem
1.1
. The previous reduction broke the Hamiltonian structure of the system. Therefore, a first step is to recover the complex Hamiltonian formulation through a symplectic correction. The corresponding result is stated in
Proposition

7.1
and serves as the foundation for the subsequent normal form analysis. Then, in the technical subsection
7.1
, we introduce the class of super-action preserving Hamiltonians that play a central role in the forthcoming analysis, as they do not contribute to the energy estimate. Next, in
Section

7.2
, we perform a Birkhoff normal form procedure, which extracts the Hamiltonian super-action preserving property up to homogeneity
N+1
, for a given fixed integer
N
. Finally, we implement in
Section

7.3
the energy estimate allowing to conclude the desired Theorem.

The transformation
{\bm{B}}(U)
in Proposition
6.1
destroyed the Hamiltonian property of the system. However
{\bm{B}}(U)
was still linearly symplectic and therefore, we can recover the complex Hamiltonian structure by applying the abstract Darboux Theorem of Berti-Maspero-Murgante
[
27
, Theorem 7.1]
, see also
Theorem

A.16
. The corresponding result states as follows.

Proposition 7.1

(
Hamiltonian reduction up to smoothing operators).

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\varrho>{\varrho(N)}\triangleq 3(N+1)+\frac{3}{2}(N+1)^{3}
.
Then, for any
K\geqslant\underline{K}^{\prime}
(fixed in Proposition
6.1
)
there is
s_{0}>0,\epsilon_{0}>0
, such that
for any solution
U\in B_{s_{0},{\mathbb{R}}}^{K}(I;\epsilon_{0})

of (
5.9
), there exists a real-to-real matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators
\boldsymbol{R}(U)
in
\left(\Sigma_{1}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}

for any
\varrho^{\prime}\geqslant 0
, such that defining

Z_{0}\triangleq\big({\rm Id}+\bm{R}(\Phi(U))\big)\Phi(U),\qquad\Phi(U)\triangleq\bm{B}(U;t)U,

(7.1)

where
\bm{B}(U;t)
is defined in Proposition
6.1
,
the following holds true:

i

Symplecticity:
The non-linear map
\big({\rm Id}+\bm{R}(\cdot)\big)\circ\Phi

in (
7.1
)
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
according to Definition
A.11
.

ii

Conjugation:

The variable
Z_{0}
solves the Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
(cfr. Definition
A.10
).

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{0}

\displaystyle={\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)Z_{0}+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0};\xi)+{\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)Z_{0}+\boldsymbol{R}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})Z_{0}+\boldsymbol{R}_{>N}(U;t)U,

(7.2)

where

•

\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)\triangleq\OpvecPlain\left(\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\xi)\right)
is a matrix in the space
\left(\Gamma^{3/2}_{0}\right)^{2\times 2}
;

•

(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leqslant N}
is a pluri-homogeneous, real valued symbol, independent of
x
, in

\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
;

•

(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}
is a non–homogeneous symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
with imaginary part

\textnormal{Im}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
;

•

\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in

\left(\Sigma_{1}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho+\varrho(N)}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
;

•

\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\left(\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+\varrho(N)}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

iii

Boundedness:

The variable
Z_{0}={\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
with
{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)\in\left(\mathcal{M}^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime}-1,0}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
and
for any
s\geqslant s_{0}
, there is
0<\epsilon_{0}(s)<\epsilon_{0}
, such that
for any
U\in B_{s_{0}}^{K}(I;\epsilon_{0})\cap C_{*{\mathbb{R}}}^{K}(I;\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2}))
,
there is a constant
C\triangleq C_{s,K}>0
such that, for all
k=0,\dots,K-\underline{K}^{\prime}
,

C^{-1}\|U\|_{k,s}\leqslant\|Z_{0}\|_{k,s}\leqslant C\|U\|_{k,s}\,.

(7.3)

7.1
Super-action preserving symbols and Hamiltonians

In this section we define the special class of “super–action preserving"

{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}

homogeneous symbols and Hamiltonians
which will appear in the Birkhoff normal form reduction of the
Section
7.2
.

Definition 7.2
.

(
{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}
monomial)

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
. Given
(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})=(j_{a},\sigma_{a})_{a=1,\dots,p}\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{*})^{p}\times\{\pm\}^{p}
we define the multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}
with components, for any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})\triangleq\#\big\{a=1,\ldots,p\,:\,(j_{a},\sigma_{a})=(k,+)\big\}\,,

(7.4)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})\triangleq\#\big\{a=1,\ldots,p\,:\,(j_{a},\sigma_{a})=(k,-)\big\}\,.

We say that a monomial of the form

z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}=z_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}\dots z_{j_{p}}^{\sigma_{p}}
is super-action preserving if the associated multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}),\beta(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}))
is super-action preserving according to Definition
2.5
.

We now introduce the subset
\mathfrak{S}_{p}
of the indexes of
\mathfrak{T}_{p}

composed by super-action preserving indexes

\mathfrak{S}_{p}\triangleq\Big\{(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}\quad\textnormal{s.t.}\quad(\alpha(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}),\beta(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}))\in{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}\ \text{in}\ \eqref{defalbe}\ \text{are super action preserving}\Big\}\,.

(7.5)

We remark that the multi-index
(\alpha,\beta)
associated to

(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{*}\times\{\pm\})^{p}
as in (
7.4
) satisfies
|\alpha+\beta|=p
and

z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}=z^{\alpha}\bar{z}^{\beta}\triangleq\prod_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}}z_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}{\overline{z_{j}}}^{\beta_{j}}=\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}z_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}z_{-n}^{\alpha_{-n}}{\overline{z_{n}}}^{\beta_{n}}\overline{z_{-n}}^{\beta_{-n}}\,\,.

(7.6)

It turns out

\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\vec{\jmath})=\sigma_{1}{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}({j_{1}})+\dots+\sigma_{p}{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}({j_{p}})=(\alpha-\beta)\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}(\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k}){\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(k)\,,

(7.7)

where we denote

\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}({\vec{\jmath}})\triangleq({\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}({j_{1}}),\dots,{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}({j_{p}})),\qquad\vec{{\omega}}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\triangleq\{{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j)\}_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}.

(7.8)

Remark 7.3
.

If the monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}
is super–action preserving then, for any
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
, the monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}
is super-action preserving as well.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
, we define the
super-action

J_{n}\triangleq|z_{n}|^{2}+|z_{-n}|^{2}.

(7.9)

The following is Lemma 7.7 in
[
27
]
.

Lemma 7.4
.

The Poisson bracket between a monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}

and a super-action
J_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, defined
in (
7.9
), is

\left\{z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}},J_{n}\right\}={\rm i}\big(\beta_{n}+\beta_{-n}-\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{-n}\big)\,z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}\,,

(7.10)

where
(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}),\beta(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma}))
is the multi-index defined in (
7.4
).
In particular a super-action preserving
monomial
z_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}

(according to Definition
7.2
) Poisson commutes with any super-action
J_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

We now define a super-action preserving Hamiltonian.

Definition 7.5
.

(
{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}
Hamiltonian)
Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
A
(p+2)
–homogeneous super-action preserving Hamiltonian
H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{p+2}(Z)
is a real function of the form

H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{p+2}(Z)=\frac{1}{p+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{\jmath}_{p+2},\vec{\sigma}_{p+2})\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p+2}\end{subarray}}H_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+2}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+2}}

where
\mathfrak{S}_{p+2}
is defined as in (
7.5
).
A pluri-homogeneous super-action preserving Hamiltonian is a finite sum of
homogeneous super-action preserving Hamiltonians.
A Hamiltonian vector field is super-action preserving if it is generated by a
super-action preserving Hamiltonian.

We now define a super-action preserving symbol.

Definition 7.6
.

(
{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}
symbol)

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
For
p\geqslant 1
a real valued,
p
–homogeneous
super-action preserving symbol
of order
m
is a symbol
{\mathtt{m}}_{p}^{({{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}})}(Z;\xi)
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
, independent of
x
, of the form

{\mathtt{m}}_{p}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}(Z;\xi)=\sum_{({\vec{\jmath}_{p}},{\vec{\sigma}_{p}})\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}}M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}\,.

For
p=0
we say that any symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{0}
is
super-action preserving.
A pluri-homogeneous super-action preserving symbol is a finite sum of
homogeneous super-action preserving symbols.

Remark 7.7
.

A super-action preserving symbol has even degree
p
of homogeneity.
Indeed, if
z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}
is super-
action preserving then
(\alpha,\beta)
defined in (
7.4
) satisfies

|\alpha|=|\beta|
and
p=|\alpha+\beta|=2|\alpha|
is even.

Given a super-action preserving symbol we associate a
super-action preserving Hamiltonian according to the following lemma (see Lemma 7.11 in
[
27
]
).

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
If
({\mathtt{m}}^{({{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}})})_{p}(Z;\xi)
is a
p
–homogeneous super-action preserving symbol
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
according to Definition
7.6
then

H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{p+2}(Z)\triangleq\textnormal{Re}\left\langle{\OpBWPlain\left(({\mathtt{m}}^{({{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}})})_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)}z\ \middle|\ \bar{z}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}}}

is a
(p+2)
–homogeneous super-action preserving Hamiltonian according to Definition
7.5
.

7.2
Birkhoff normal form reduction

In this section we finally
transform the system (
7.2
) into its
Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form, up to homogeneity
N
.

Proposition 7.9
.

(Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form)

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
0<\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<4\left(2+\sqrt{3}\right)
.
Assume that
the parameter
\beta=\frac{{\mathtt{b}}^{2}}{\gamma}\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]
is outside the zero measure set

{\cal B}\subset\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]
defined in Proposition
2.6
. Then, there exists
\underline{\varrho}=\underline{\varrho}(N)>0
such that, for any
\varrho\geqslant\underline{\varrho}
, for any
K\geqslant K^{\prime}\triangleq\underline{K}^{\prime}(\varrho)
(defined in Proposition
6.1
),
there exists
\underline{s}_{0}>0
such that, for any
s\geqslant\underline{s}_{0}
there is
\underline{\epsilon}_{0}\triangleq\underline{\epsilon}_{0}(s)>0
such that for all
0<\epsilon_{0}<\underline{\epsilon}_{0}(s)
small enough, and any solution
U\in B_{\underline{s}_{0}}^{K}(I;\epsilon_{0})\cap C^{K}_{*{\mathbb{R}}}(I;\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2}))
of the complex Kelvin-Helmholtz system (
5.9
),
there exists a
non–linear map
\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})

such that:

i

Symplecticity:

\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
A.11
);

ii

Conjugation:
If
Z_{0}
solves the system (
7.2
) then the variable
Z\triangleq\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})
solves the Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
(cfr. Definition
A.10
)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z

\displaystyle={{\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)}Z+\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}(Z)+\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}(Z)+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)Z+\boldsymbol{R}_{>N}(U;t)U,

(7.11)

where

•

H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}(Z)
is the super-action preserving Hamiltonian

\textnormal{Re}\left\langle{\OpBWPlain\left((\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})})_{\leqslant N}(Z;\xi)\right)}z\ \middle|\ \bar{z}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}}},

with a pluri-homogeneous super-action preserving symbol

(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})})_{\leqslant N}(Z;\xi)
in
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{q}^{\frac{3}{2}}
,
according to Definition
7.6
;

•

{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}(Z)

is a super-action preserving, Hamiltonian, smoothing vector field in
\Sigma_{3}^{N+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}}_{q}
(see
7.5
);

•

(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
is a non–homogeneous symbol in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
with imaginary part
\textnormal{Im}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
;

•

\boldsymbol{R}_{>N}(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators
in
\left(\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

iii

Boundedness:
There exists a constant
C\triangleq C_{s,K}>0
such that for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant K
and any

Z_{0}\in B_{\underline{s}_{0}}^{K}(I;\epsilon_{0})\cap C^{K}_{*{\mathbb{R}}}(I;\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{T}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2}))
one has

C^{-1}\|Z_{0}\|_{k,s}\leqslant\|\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{nf}}(Z_{0})\|_{k,s}\leqslant C\|Z_{0}\|_{k,s}

(7.12)

and

{C}^{-1}\|U(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leqslant\|Z(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\leqslant C\|U(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\,,\quad\forall t\in I.

(7.13)

Notation 7.10
.

From now on we denote with
\bm{R}_{p}^{\mathtt{H}}
any smoothing remainder in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho}_{p}
such that
\bm{R}_{p}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z)Z
is a
p+1
-homogeneous Hamiltonian vector field, namely
\bm{R}_{p}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z)Z=\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H_{p+2}(Z)
for some
p+2
homogeneous Hamiltonian as per
Definition

A.8
.

Proof of
Proposition

7.9
.

The proof consists in
N
steps and is analogous to the proof of Proposition 7.12 in
[
27
]
. For completeness, we only sketch the main steps and we refer to
[
27
]
for a detailed proof.

We first reduce the quadratic terms of the vector field in (
7.2
).

Step
1
: Elimination of the quadratic smoothing remainder in equation (
7.2
).

The
x
-independent symbol
(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0};\xi)
in (
7.2
) belongs to
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
and the only quadratic component of the vector field in (
7.2
) is

\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
where (recall the notation in (
3.18
))

\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})\triangleq{\mathcal{P}}_{1}[{\bm{R}}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})]\in\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{-\varrho+\varrho(N)}\right)^{2\times 2}.

(7.14)

Since the system (
7.2
) is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
,
\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
is a Hamiltonian vector field
that we expand in Fourier coordinates as (recall (
3.19
))

\big(\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}\big)_{k}^{\sigma}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in\mathfrak{T}_{3}\end{subarray}}X_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}(z_{0})_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}(z_{0})_{j_{2}}^{\sigma_{2}}.

(7.15)

In order to remove
\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
from (
7.2
),
we perform the change of variable

Z_{1}=\mathtt{F}^{(1)}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})

where
\mathtt{F}^{(1)}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})

is the map given by Lemma
A.17
relative to
a Hamiltonian smoothing vector field

\big({\bm{G}}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}\big)_{k}^{\sigma}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}\end{subarray}}G_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}(z_{0})_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}(z_{0})_{j_{2}}^{\sigma_{2}},\quad\bm{G}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})\in\left({\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{1}\right)^{2\times 2}

(7.16)

for some
\varrho^{\prime}>0
to be determined.
Since
\bm{G}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z)Z
is a Hamiltonian vector field,
Lemma
A.17
gives by construction that
\mathtt{F}^{(1)}_{\leqslant N}
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
and it has the form

Z_{1}\triangleq\mathtt{F}^{(1)}_{\leqslant N}(Z_{0})=Z_{0}+{\bm{G}}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}+\bm{F}_{\geqslant 2}(Z_{0})Z_{0},\qquad\bm{F}_{\geqslant 2}(Z_{0})\in\left(\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}.

(7.17)

Applying Lemma
A.12
, we obtain that the variable
Z_{1}
solves a system which is
Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.
We compute it using Lemma
B.2
.
Its assumption
(A)
at page
B
holds since
Z_{0}
solves (
7.2
).
Then
Lemma
B.2
implies that the variable
Z_{1}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{1}

\displaystyle={\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)Z_{1}+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leqslant N}^{+}(Z_{1};\xi)+{\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}^{+}(U;t,\xi)\right)Z_{1}

(7.18)

\displaystyle+[\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{1})+\bm{G}^{+}_{1}(Z_{1})]Z_{1}+R_{\geqslant 2}^{+}(Z_{1})Z_{1}+R^{+}_{>N}(U;t)U,

where

\bullet

(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\leqslant N}^{+}(Z_{1};\xi)
is a real valued symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{q}^{\frac{3}{2}}
;

\bullet

(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}^{+}(U;t,\xi)
is a non-homogeneous real valued symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
with imaginary part
{\rm Im}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
;

\bullet

\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{1})
is defined in (
7.14
) and
\bm{G}_{1}^{+}(Z_{1})Z_{1}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}+\frac{3}{2}}_{2}
has Fourier expansion, by (
B.15
) and (
7.16
),

(\bm{G}^{+}_{1}(Z_{1})Z_{1})_{k}^{\sigma}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm i}\big(\sigma_{1}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j_{1})+\sigma_{2}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j_{2})-\sigma\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(k)\big)G_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}(z_{1})_{j_{1}}^{\sigma_{1}}(z_{1})_{j_{2}}^{\sigma_{2}};

(7.19)

\bullet

\bm{R}_{\geqslant 2}^{+}(Z_{1})
is a matrix of pluri-homogeneous smoothing operators in
\left(\Sigma_{2}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(2)}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
where

-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(2)\triangleq-\varrho^{\prime}+\frac{3}{2}\ ;

(7.20)

\bullet

\bm{R}_{>N}^{+}(U;t)
is a matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\left(\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(2)}_{K,\underline{K}^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

By (
7.15
), (
7.19
) we solve

\bm{R}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{1})Z_{1}+\bm{G}_{1}^{+}(Z_{1})Z_{1}=0

(7.21)

by taking

G_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}\triangleq\begin{cases}0,&\mbox{ if }\ (j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\notin{\mathfrak{T}}_{3},\\
\displaystyle-{\frac{X_{j_{1},j_{2},k}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma}}{{\rm i}\big(\sigma_{1}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(j_{1})+\sigma_{2}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}({j_{2}})-\sigma\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}({k})\big)}},&\mbox{ if }(j_{1},j_{2},k,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{3}\,.\end{cases}

(7.22)

The previous expression and the fact that the vector field
{\bm{R}}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
is Hamiltonian justifies a posteriori that indeed
{\bm{G}}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}
is Hamiltonian. Moreover, combining (
7.14
) and Proposition
2.6
, taking
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus{\mathcal{B}}
, we get that
{\bm{G}}_{1}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{0})Z_{0}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{2}
with
\varrho^{\prime}\triangleq\varrho-\varrho(N)-\tau
. Thus, by
Eq.

7.20
, we get
\underline{\varrho}(2)=\varrho(N)+\tau+\frac{3}{2}
.

Step
p\geqslant 2
:

We now assume that the system is in normal form up to degree
p-1
of homogeneity. Next, we illustrate how to perform the normal form transformation on the terms of degree
p
. Specifically, we assume that
Z_{p-1}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z_{p-1}

\displaystyle={\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)Z_{p-1}+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}(Z_{p-1})+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}(Z_{p-1})

\displaystyle\quad+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}(Z_{p-1};\xi)+{\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\geqslant p+1}(Z_{p-1};\xi)\right)Z_{p-1}+{\bm{R}}_{\geqslant p}^{\mathtt{H}}(Z_{p-1})Z_{p-1}

\displaystyle\quad+\OpvecPlain\left(-{\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)Z_{p-1}+\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)U,

(7.23)

where
\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}
,
\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}
are pluri-homogeneous super-action preserving Hamiltonians,
(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{\frac{3}{2}}
and expands as

(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}(Z_{p-1};\xi)=\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}}{\mathtt{D}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)\,(z_{p-1})_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}},\qquad\overline{{\mathtt{D}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{-\vec{\sigma}_{p}}}(\xi)={\mathtt{D}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi).

(7.24)

Moreover
(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\geqslant(p+1)}\in\Sigma_{p+1}^{N}\Gamma_{q}^{\frac{3}{2}}
and
\bm{R}_{\geqslant p}^{\mathtt{H}}\in\Sigma_{p}^{N}{\mathcal{R}}_{q}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(p)}
. We reduce the homogeneous component

\OpvecPlain\left(-{\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}\right)+R_{p}
into its resonant normal form. First of all we define the intermediate variable

W\triangleq\Phi_{p}(Z_{p-1})\triangleq\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}^{1}(Z_{p-1})Z_{p-1},

(7.25)

where
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}^{1}(Z_{p-1})
is
the time
1
-linear flow generated by
\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}g_{p}\right)
,
where
g_{p}
is the Fourier multiplier

g_{p}(Z_{p-1};\xi)\triangleq\sum\limits_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}}{\mathtt{G}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)(z_{p-1})_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

(7.26)

Then
Lemma
B.1
implies that the
variable
W
defined in (
7.25
) solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W

\displaystyle={\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)W+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}(W)+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}(W)

(7.27)

\displaystyle+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}[(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}(W;\xi)+g^{+}_{p}(W;\xi)]+{\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\geqslant p+1}^{+}(W;\xi)\right)W+\bm{R}_{\geqslant p}(W)W

\displaystyle+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}^{+}(U;t,\xi)\right)W+\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)U,

where
g_{p}^{+}
expands as

\displaystyle g^{+}_{p}(W;\xi)=\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}}

\displaystyle{\rm i}\vec{\sigma}_{p}\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\vec{\jmath}_{p}){\mathtt{G}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)w_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}},

(7.28)

while
\bm{R}_{\geqslant p}\in\left(\Sigma_{1}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(p)+c(N,p)}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
,
\bm{R}_{>N}\in\left(\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(p)+c(N,p)}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
for some
c(N,p)>0
. In order to get a
p
-homogeneous super-action preserving normal form symbol, we solve the homological equation

\displaystyle(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}(W;\xi)+g^{+}_{p}(W;\xi)=(\mathpzc{d}^{({{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}})}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}(W;\xi)\triangleq\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}}{\mathtt{D}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)\,w_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}},

where
\mathfrak{S}_{p}
has been introduced in (
7.5
). One solution is obtained by choosing

\displaystyle{\mathtt{G}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)\triangleq\begin{cases}0,&\mbox{ if }(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in\mathfrak{S}_{p},\\
\displaystyle{\frac{{\mathtt{D}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)}{{\rm i}\vec{\sigma}_{p}\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\vec{\jmath}_{p})}},&\mbox{ if }(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\not\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}.\end{cases}

(7.29)

The previous expression (
7.29
) and the fact that
(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}
is real valued justifies a posteriori that indeed
g_{p}
is real valued. Moreover, combining estimate (
3.5
) for
(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}
and Proposition
2.6
, taking
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus{\mathcal{B}}
, we get that
g_{p}
satisfies the corresponding estimate (
3.5
) with
\mu\leadsto\mu+\tau
. Now, we observe that, by Lemma
A.13
,

\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{p}(W;\xi)\right)W={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H_{\frac{3}{2}}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}\big)_{p+2}(W)+\bm{R}_{p}(W)W,

(7.30)

with Hamiltonian

\big(H_{\frac{3}{2}}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}\big)_{p+2}(W)\triangleq\textnormal{Re}\Big\langle{\OpBWPlain\left((\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}}^{({{\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}}})})_{p}(W;\xi)\right)}w,\bar{w}\Big\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}}}\ ,

(7.31)

which is super-action preserving by Lemma
7.8
,
and a matrix of smoothing operators
\bm{R}_{p}(W)
in
\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
for any
\varrho^{\prime}\geqslant 0
.
Therefore (
7.27
) becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)W+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big)_{\leqslant p+2}(W)+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}(W)

(7.32)

\displaystyle+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{\geqslant p+1}^{+}(W;\xi)\right)W+[\bm{R}_{\geqslant p}(W)]W

\displaystyle+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}^{+}(U;t,\xi)\right)W+\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)U,

where (see (
7.23
),(
7.31
))

\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big)_{\leqslant p+2}\triangleq\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}+\big(H_{\frac{3}{2}}^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}\big)_{p+2},

(7.33)

while
\bm{R}_{\geqslant p}
is a new smoothing remainder in
\left(\Sigma_{1}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(p)+c(N,p)}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
.
Note that the new system (
7.32
) is not Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
(unlike system (
7.23
) for
Z_{p-1}
), since the map

\Phi_{p}(Z_{p-1})=\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{1}_{g_{p}}(Z_{p-1})Z_{p-1}
in
(
7.25
) is not symplectic up to homogeneity
N
. By Lemma
A.7
we only know
that
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{1}_{g_{p}}(Z_{p-1})
is linearly symplectic.
We now apply Theorem
A.16
to find a correction of
\Phi_{p}(Z_{p-1})
which is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
. The assumptions of Theorem
A.16
are verified applying Lemma
A.7
, therefore there exists
\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}^{(p)}\in\left(\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho-\frac{3}{2}}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
such that the variable

V\triangleq{\mathcal{C}}_{N}^{(p)}(W)\triangleq\big({\rm Id}+\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}^{(p)}(W)\big)W=\big({\rm Id}+\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}^{(p)}(\Phi_{p}(Z_{p-1}))\big)\Phi_{p}(Z_{p-1})

(7.34)

is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, thus
solves a system which is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
. Since
W
solves (
7.32
) then Lemma
B.2
implies that
V=W+{\bm{R}}_{\leqslant N}^{(p)}(W)W
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)V+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}\big)_{\leqslant p+2}(V)+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\big(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}\big)_{\leqslant p+1}(V)

(7.35)

\displaystyle+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}\widetilde{(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})}_{\geqslant p+1}(V;\xi)\right)V+\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p+2}(V)+\bm{R}_{\geqslant p+1}(V)V

\displaystyle+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}\widetilde{(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})}_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)V+\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)U,

where
\widetilde{(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})}_{\geqslant p+1}\in\Sigma_{p+1}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
,
\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p+2}\in\mathfrak{X}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(p)+C(N,p)}_{p}
,
\bm{R}_{\geqslant p+1}\in\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\varrho+\underline{\varrho}(p)+C(N,p)}
. Notice that the Hamiltonian structure of
\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p}(V)
is justified a posteriori by the fact that the
p
-homogeneous component of the vector field in (
7.35
) is Hamiltonian and the term in its first line is Hamiltonian, thus we deduce the Hamiltonianity by difference. Thus we can proceed as in Step
1
. First we Fourier expand
\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p+2}
as

\big(\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p+2}(V)\big)_{k}^{\sigma}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}v_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}\,.

(7.36)

Then we use Lemma
A.17
to generate a symplectic up to homogeneity
N
map
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leqslant N}^{(p)}
associate to the Hamiltonian smoothing vector field

\big(\bm{G}_{p}(V)V\big)_{k}^{\sigma}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}v_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}.

(7.37)

Applying Lemma
B.2
the analogue of the homological equation (
7.21
) is

\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p+2}(Z_{p})+\bm{G}_{p}^{+}(Z_{p})Z_{p}={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}\right)_{p}(Z_{p})\triangleq\!\!\!\sum_{{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p+2}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}\,(z_{p})_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}

(7.38)

where the vector field
\bm{G}_{p}^{+}(Z_{p})Z_{p}
is explicitly given by

(\bm{G}_{p}^{+}(Z_{p})Z_{p})_{k}^{\sigma}\triangleq\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm i}\big(\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1})-\sigma\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(k)\big)G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}(z_{p})_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}\ .

Then a solution of (
7.38
) is given by

\displaystyle G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}\triangleq\begin{cases}0,&\mbox{ if }(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+2},\\
-\displaystyle{\frac{X_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}}{{\rm i}(\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1})-\sigma\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(k))}},&\mbox{ if }(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\not\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+2}.\end{cases}

(7.39)

The previous expression and the fact that the vector field
\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p+2}
is Hamiltonian justifies a posteriori that indeed
G_{p}(Z_{p})Z_{p}
is Hamiltonian. Moreover, since
\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla\left(H_{-\varrho}\right)_{p+2}
is
\varrho_{p}
-smoothing, we apply Proposition
2.6
, taking
\beta\in\left[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\right]\setminus{\mathcal{B}}
and we get that
\bm{G}_{p}(Z_{p})Z_{p}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho_{p+1}}_{2}
with
\varrho_{p+1}\triangleq\varrho_{p}-c(N)-\tau.

∎

7.3
Energy estimate and proof of the main Theorem

Remark 7.11
.

We can derive local existence for (
5.9
) following the computations in the main result of
[
25
]
and exploiting the fact that the local existence result of
[
25
]
uses the very same paradifferential functional setting as in the present manuscript. Minor modifications in the works
[
3
,
53
]
can also provide local existence for the setting of (
1.4
).

The following result, analogous to
[
21
, Lemma 6.3]
,
enables to bound the norms
\|\partial_{t}^{k}U(t)\|_{s-\frac{3}{2}k}
of the time derivatives of a solution

U(t)
of (
5.9
) by
\|U(t)\|_{s}
.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
. There exists
s_{0}>0
such that for any
s\geqslant s_{0}
, any
\epsilon\in\left(0,\overline{\epsilon_{0}}\left(s\right)\right)
small, if
U
belongs to

B^{0}_{s_{0},{\mathbb{R}}}\left(I;\epsilon\right)\cap C^{0}_{\ast}\left(I;\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\right)\right)

and solves (
5.2
) then
U\in C^{K}_{*{\mathbb{R}}}\left(I;\dot{H}^{s}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\right)
and there exists
C_{1}\triangleq C_{1}\left(s,K\right)\geqslant 1
such that

\left\|U\left(t\right)\right\|_{s}\leqslant\left\|U\left(t\right)\right\|_{K,s}\leqslant C_{1}\left\|U\left(t\right)\right\|_{s},\qquad\forall t\in I.

Proof.

The proof is inductive and follows the same line of
[
21
, Lemma 6.3]
, so we sketch only the first step. As
\|U(t)\|_{s}\leqslant\|U\left(t\right)\|_{K,s}
is obvious in view of the definition (
3.3
), it remains to prove the second inequality. We start by estimating
\|\partial_{t}U(t)\|_{s-\frac{3}{2}}
. Using equation (
5.2
) for
U(t)
and then (
3.13
) with
k=K^{\prime}=p=0
,
m=\frac{3}{2}
and estimate (
3.16
) with
m\leadsto-\varrho
and
k=0
, we get

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}U(t)\|_{s-\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}_{\frac{3}{2}}\left(U;x\right)\ \omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}\left(\xi\right)+{\bm{A}}_{1}\left(U;x,\xi\right)+{\bm{A}}_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(U;x\right)\ \left|\xi\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}+{\bm{A}}_{\left[0\right]}\left(U;x,\xi\right)\right)}U\right\|_{s-\frac{3}{2}}+\left\|{\bm{R}}\left(U\right)U\right\|_{s-\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|U(t)\|_{s}.

The estimates for
k\geqslant 2
follow in a similar manner, additionally using estimates (
3.13
) and (
3.16
) with
k\geqslant 1
to handle higher-order derivatives.

∎

We fix now the parameters appearing in
Proposition

7.9
.
In its statement, we set
\varrho\triangleq\underline{\varrho}(N)
and
K\triangleq\underline{K^{\prime}}(\varrho)
,
which implies the existence of the constant
\underline{s_{0}}>0
which we increase, if necessary, to fit the range of
Lemma

7.12
, such that for any
s\geqslant\underline{s_{0}}
, and any fixed

0<\epsilon_{0}\leqslant\min\{\overline{\epsilon_{0}}(s),\underline{\epsilon_{0}}(s)\}
,
where
\underline{\epsilon_{0}}(s)
is defined in
Proposition

7.9
,
and
\overline{\epsilon_{0}}(s)
in
Lemma

7.12
, the conclusions of
Proposition

7.9
and
Lemma

7.12
hold. Therefore, one can obtain the following energy estimate. Notice that the time-reversibility of the Kelvin-Helmholtz system allows us to restrict the discussion to positive times
t>0.

Lemma 7.13

(Energy estimate)
.

Let
U(t)
be a solution of
equation (
5.9
) in

B^{K}_{\underline{s_{0}}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}\right)\cap C^{K}_{\ast}\left(I;\dot{H}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\right)\right)
.
Then there exists
\bar{C}_{2}\left(s\right)>1
such that

\left\|U\left(t\right)\right\|_{s}^{2}\leqslant\bar{C}_{2}\left(s\right)\left(\left\|U\left(0\right)\right\|_{s}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|U\left(\tau\right)\right\|_{s_{0}}^{N+1}\left\|U\left(\tau\right)\right\|_{s}^{2}\textnormal{d}\tau\right),\quad\forall 0<t<T.

(7.40)

Proof.

The variable
Z
defined in
Proposition

7.9
solves the normal form
Equation

7.11
. Then, denoting by
H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}\triangleq H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{\frac{3}{2}}+H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}_{-\varrho}
we have that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left\|Z(t)\right\|_{s}^{2}=

\displaystyle\ {\rm d}_{Z}\left\|Z\right\|^{2}_{s}\left[{\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)Z+\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}+{\OpBWPlain\left({\rm i}(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}})_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)}Z+\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)U\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left|n\right|^{2s}\left\{J_{n}\big.,\ H^{({\mathtt{S}\mathtt{A}\mathtt{P}})}\right\}

\displaystyle+\left\langle\left|D\right|^{2s}{\OpBWPlain\left(-\textnormal{Im}\left(\left(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}}\right)_{>N}\left(U;t,\xi\right)\right)\right)}Z\ \middle|\ Z\right\rangle+2\textnormal{Re}\left\langle\left|D\right|^{2s}{\bm{R}}_{>N}\left(U;t\right)U\ \middle|\ Z\right\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\ \left\|U\right\|_{K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{N+1}\left(\left\|Z\right\|_{s}+\left\|U\right\|_{s}\right)\left\|Z\right\|_{s},

where we used
Lemma

7.4
and the fact that
-\textnormal{Im}\left(\left(\mathpzc{d}_{\frac{3}{2}}\right)_{>N}\left(U;t,\xi\right)\right)
is in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
and
{\bm{R}}_{>N}\left(U;t\right)
is in
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
. Integrating in time the above inequality, then using
Eqs.

7.13
and
7.12
, we obtain (
7.40
).
∎

The energy estimate (
7.40
)
and the equivalence

\left\|\eta\left(t\right)\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{4}}_{0}}+\left\|\psi\left(t\right)\right\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{4}}}\sim\left\|U\left(t\right)\right\|_{s},

(7.41)

which is a consequence of (
5.8
),
imply, by a standard
bootstrap argument,
Theorem

1.1
. For detailed computations we refer the interested reader to
[
20
,
27
,
21
]
.

Appendix A
Hamiltonian formalism

In this appendix we set the Hamiltonian formalism for the problem at hand, following
[
27
, Section 3]
.

A.1
Linearly Hamiltonian systems

In this section we provide the linear Hamiltonian framework needed in our analysis. The linear Hamiltonian structure is the natural algebraic property that emerges from the paralinearization of an Hamiltonian system.

Definition A.1

(Linearly Hamiltonian paradifferential operator)
.

A real-to-real matrix of paradifferential complex operators is
linearly Hamiltonian
if it is of the form

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{B}}\right)},\qquad{\bm{B}}\triangleq\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}b_{1}\left(U;t,x,\xi\right)&b_{2}\left(U;t,x,\xi\right)\\
\overline{b_{2}\left(U;t,x,-\xi\right)}&\overline{b_{1}\left(U;t,x,-\xi\right)}\end{array}\right],

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &b_{1}\left(U;t,x,-\xi\right)=b_{1}\left(U;t,x,\xi\right),\\
&b_{2}\left(U;t,x,\xi\right)\in\mathbb{R}.\end{aligned}\right.

In other words,
b_{1}
is even in
\xi
and
b_{2}
is real valued. This is equivalent to require that the matrix valued paradifferential operator
{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{B}}\right)}
is symmetric.

Definition A.2

(Linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N
)
.

A real-to-real matrix of spectrally localized maps

{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\bf B}(U;t)
in
\left(\Sigma{\cal S}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]\right)^{2\times 2}

is
linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
if
its pluri-homogeneous component

{\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant N}({\bf B}(U;t))
(defined through (
3.18
)) is symmetric, namely

{\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant N}({\bf B}(U;t))={\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant N}\left({\bf B}(U;t)^{\intercal}\right).

In particular, a real-to-real matrix of paradifferential operators is
linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
if it has the form (cfr. (
A.1
))

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left(\begin{matrix}b_{1}(U;t,x,\xi)&b_{2}(U;t,x,\xi)\\
\overline{b_{2}(U;t,x,-\xi)}&\overline{b_{1}(U;t,x,-\xi)}\end{matrix}\right)},

\displaystyle\ \begin{cases}b_{1}(U;t,x,-\xi)-b_{1}(U;t,x,\xi)\in\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}],\\
\textnormal{Im}b_{2}(U;t,x,\xi)\in\Gamma^{m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\end{cases}

(A.3)

for some
m,m^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Definition A.3

(Linearly symplectic map)
.

A real-to-real linear transformation
{\mathcal{A}}
is linearly symplectic if
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}=\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
,
where
\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}
is defined as

\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}u_{1}\\
\bar{u}_{1}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}u_{2}\\
\bar{u}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)\triangleq\left\langle{\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}\begin{bmatrix}u_{1}\\
\bar{u}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\ \middle|\ \begin{bmatrix}u_{2}\\
\bar{u}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}},\qquad{\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}\triangleq{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1},

(A.4)

namely

{\mathcal{A}}^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{A}}={\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Definition A.4
.

(Linearly symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
)

A real-to-real matrix of spectrally localized maps
{\bf S}(U;t)
in
\left(\Sigma{\mathcal{S}}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[r,N]\right)^{2\times 2}

is
linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
if

{\bf S}(U;t)^{\top}\,{\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}\,{\bf S}(U;t)={\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}+S_{>N}(U;t),

(A.5)

where
{\bm{E}}_{\bf C}
is the symplectic operator defined in (
A.4
) and

S_{>N}(U;t)
is a
matrix of spectrally localized maps
in
\left({\mathcal{S}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

Linearly symplectic maps up to homogeneity
N
preserve the linear Hamiltonian structure up to homogeneity
N
. The following result is borrowed from
[
27
, Lemma 3.9]
.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\bm{B}}(U;t)
be a linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N

(Definition
A.2
)
and
{\bm{S}}(U;t)
be an invertible map, linearly symplectic to homogeneity
N
(Definition
A.4
).
Then the operators

{\bm{S}}(U;t){\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\bm{B}}(U;t)\bm{S}(U;t)^{-1}
and

(\partial_{t}{\bm{S}}(U;t)){\bm{S}}^{-1}(U;t)

are linearly Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.

We consider the flow of a linearly
Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
paradifferential operator. The following is Lemma 3.16 of
[
27
]
.

Lemma A.6
.

(Linear symplectic flow)

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
N,K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K^{\prime}\leqslant K
,
m\leqslant 1
,
r>0
. Let

{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{B}}\right)}={\OpBWPlain\left(\begin{bmatrix}\overline{{\rm i}b_{2}^{\vee}}&\overline{{\rm i}b_{1}^{\vee}}\\
{\rm i}b_{1}&{\rm i}b_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)}

be a linearly Hamiltonian operator up to homogeneity
N

(Definition
A.2
)
where
{\bm{B}}
is a matrix of symbols

{\bm{B}}\triangleq{\bm{B}}(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)&b_{2}(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)\\
\overline{b_{2}(\tau,U;t,x,-\xi)}&\overline{b_{1}(\tau,U;t,x,-\xi)}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\begin{cases}b_{1}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N],\\
b_{2}\in\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N],\end{cases}

with

b_{1}^{\vee}-b_{1}
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
and the imaginary part

\textup{ Im }b_{2}
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
(cfr. (
A.3
)) uniformly in
|\tau|\leqslant 1
.
Then there exists
s_{0}>0
such that, for any
U\in B_{s_{0},{\mathbb{R}}}^{K}(I;r)
,
the system

\partial_{\tau}{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}}^{\tau}_{{\bm{B}}}(U;t)={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\,{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{B}}(\tau,U;t,x,\xi)\right)}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\tau}_{{\bm{B}}}(U;t),\quad\quad\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{{\bm{B}}}^{0}(U;t)=\textnormal{Id}

has a unique solution
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\tau}_{\bm{B}}(U)
defined for all
|\tau|\leqslant 1
,
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

Boundedness:
For any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
the linear map
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\tau}_{{\bm{B}}}(U;t)
is invertible and
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{{\bm{B}}}^{\tau}(U;t)
and
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{{\bm{B}}}^{\tau}(U;t)^{-1}

are non–homogeneous spectrally localized maps in
\left({\mathcal{S}}_{K,K^{\prime},0}^{0}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
according to Definition
3.20
.

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:
The map
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\tau}_{{\bm{B}}}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
A.4
).
If
{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left(\mathbf{B}\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian (Definition
A.1
), then
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\tau}_{{\bm{B}}}(U;t)
is linearly symplectic (Definition
A.3
).

(iii)

Homogeneous expansion:

\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{{\bm{B}}}^{\tau}(U;t)
and its inverse are spectrally localized maps and
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\tau}(U;t)^{\pm}-{\rm Id}
belong to

\left(\Sigma{\mathcal{S}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{(N+1)m_{0}}[r,N]\right)^{2\times 2}
with
m_{0}\triangleq\max(m,0)
, uniformly in
|\tau|\leqslant 1
.

The flow generated by a Fourier multiplier satisfies similar properties. The following is Lemma 3.17 of
[
27
]
.

Lemma A.7
.

(Flow of a Fourier multiplier)

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
g_{p}(Z;\xi)
be a
p
–homogeneous,

x
-independent, real symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{p}
. Then, the flow

\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)
defined by

\partial_{\tau}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)=\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}g_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}^{\tau}(Z)\,,\qquad\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}^{0}(Z)={\rm Id}\,,

(A.6)

is well defined for any
|\tau|\leqslant 1
and satisfies the following properties:

(i)

Boundedness:

For any
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
r>0
the flow
\,\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
and its inverse
\,\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{-\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
are real-to-real diagonal matrix of spectrally localized
maps in
\left({\mathcal{S}}_{K,0,0}^{0}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

(ii)

Linear symplecticity:

The flow map
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
is linearly symplectic (Definition
A.3
).

(iii)

Homogeneous expansion:
The flow map
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
and its inverse
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{-\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)
are matrices of spectrally localized maps
such that
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\pm\tau}_{g_{p}}(Z)-{\rm Id}
belong to
\left(\Sigma{\mathcal{S}}^{\frac{3}{2}(N+1)}_{K,0,p}[r,N]\right)^{2\times 2}
,
uniformly in
|\tau|\leqslant 1
.

A.2
Non-linear Hamiltonian systems

We first give the definition of
p
-homogeneous Hamiltonian functions.

Definition A.8

(Homogeneous Hamiltonian)
.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a
p+2
-homogeneous Hamiltonian is a function defined on
\dot{H}^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
with values in
{\mathbb{C}}
which is real valued for any
U\in\dot{H}^{\infty}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
of the form, c.f. (
3.19
)

\displaystyle H_{p+2}(U)=\langle M_{p}(U)U,U\rangle=\sum_{(\vec{\jmath},\vec{\sigma})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}H_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}\,u_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}},

\displaystyle M_{p}(U)\in\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{m}\right)^{2\times 2}

(A.7)

for some
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
and complex valued coefficients
H_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}=H_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p+2}}^{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p+2}}
. A pluri-homogeneous Hamiltonian is a polynomial of the form

H(U)=\sum_{p=0}^{N}H_{p+2}(U),

(A.8)

where, for
p=0,\ldots,N
,
H_{p+2}
is a
p+2
-homogeneous Hamiltonian.

From the definition the coefficients in (
A.7
) satisfy the following:

1.

Symmetry restrictions:
for any
\vec{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p+2})\in\left\{\pm\right\}^{p+2}
and
\vec{\jmath}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{p+2})\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{*})^{p+2},
one has

\text{{\bf Reality condition:}}\ \ \overline{H_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}}=H_{\vec{\jmath}}^{-\vec{\sigma}},\quad\quad\textbf{Momentum condition:}\ \ H_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}\neq 0\implies\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{\jmath}=0.

(A.9)

2.

Polynomial bound:
There are
\mu,C>0
and
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\left|H_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\vec{\sigma}}\right|\leqslant C{\rm max}_{3}(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p+2}|)^{\mu}{\rm max}_{2}(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p+2}|)^{m}.

Remark A.9
.

In view of the momentum condition
\vec{\sigma}\cdot\vec{\jmath}=0
in (
A.9
), one has the equivalence

{\rm max}(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p+2}|)\sim{\rm max}_{2}(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p+2}|).

We now define the class of Hamiltonian systems up to homogeneity
N
that we shall use in Section
7
.

Let
K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K^{\prime}\leqslant K
,
r>0
and
U\in B_{s_{0}}^{K}(I;r)
.
Let

Z\triangleq{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U\quad\text{ with }\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)\in\left({\mathcal{M}}^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}\,.

(A.10)

Definition A.10
.

(Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
)

Let
N,K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K\geqslant K^{\prime}+1
and
assume (
A.10
).
A
U
–dependent system

\partial_{t}Z={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H(Z)+{\bm{M}}_{>N}(U;t)[U]

(A.11)

is
Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
if

\bullet

H(Z)
is a pluri-homogeneous Hamiltonian as in (
A.8
);

\bullet

{\bm{M}}_{>N}(U;t)
is a matrix of
non-homogeneous operators in
\left({\mathcal{M}}_{K,K^{\prime}+1,N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

We shall perform nonlinear changes of variables which are symplectic up to homogeneity
N
according to the following definition.

Definition A.11
.

(Symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
)

Let
p,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
p\leqslant N
. We say that

{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t)={\bm{M}}(Z;t)Z\qquad\text{with}\qquad{\bm{M}}(Z;t)-{\rm Id}\in\left(\Sigma\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\right)^{2\times 2}\,,

(A.12)

is
symplectic up to homogeneity
N
, if its pluri-homogeneous component
{\mathcal{D}}_{\leqslant N}(Z)\triangleq\big({\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant N}M(Z;t)\big)Z
satisfies

\left[{\rm d}_{Z}{\mathcal{D}}_{\leqslant N}(Z)\right]^{\top}\,{\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}\,{\rm d}_{Z}{\mathcal{D}}_{\leqslant N}(Z)={\bm{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}+{\bm{E}}_{>N}(Z)\qquad\text{with}\qquad{\bm{E}}_{>N}(Z)\in\left(\Sigma_{N+1}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}\,.

(A.13)

A symplectic map up to homogeneity
N

transforms a Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
into
another Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
.

Lemma A.12
.

Let
p,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
p\leqslant N
,
K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K\geqslant K^{\prime}+1
.
Let
Z\triangleq{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
as in (
A.10
).
Assume
{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t)=M(Z;t)Z
is a symplectic map up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
A.11
) such that

M(Z;t)-\textnormal{Id}\in\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left(\Sigma{\mathcal{M}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\right)^{2\times 2},&\text{if}\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\\
&\left(\Sigma{\mathcal{M}}_{K,0,p}[\breve{r},N]\right)^{2\times 2}\,,&\,\forall\breve{r}>0\quad\text{otherwise}\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(A.14)

If
Z(t)

solves a
U
-dependent Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N

(Definition
A.10
),
then the variable

W\triangleq{\mathcal{D}}(Z;t)

solves another
U
-dependent Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N

(generated by the transformed Hamiltonian).

The following is Lemma 3.19 in
[
27
]
.

Lemma A.13
.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
and

a(U;x,\xi)
a real valued homogeneous
symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
.
Then the Hamiltonian vector field generated
by the Hamiltonian

H(U)\triangleq\textnormal{Re}\left\langle{\bm{A}}(U)u\ \middle|\ \bar{u}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}}},\qquad{\bm{A}}(U)\triangleq{\OpBWPlain\left(a(U;x,\xi)\right)},

is

{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H(U)=\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}a(U;x,\xi)\right)U+{\bm{R}}(U)U,

where
{\bm{R}}(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of homogeneous smoothing operators in

\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
for any
\varrho\geqslant 0
.

The following is Lemma 3.20 in
[
27
]
.

Lemma A.14
.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varrho\geqslant 0
. Let

X(U)={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}(U;x,\xi)\right)}U+{\bm{R}}(U)U={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H(U)

(A.15)

be a
(p+1)
-homogeneous Hamiltonian vector field, where

{\bm{A}}(U;x,\xi)=\begin{pmatrix}a(U;x,\xi)&b(U;x,\xi)\\
\overline{b(U;x,-\xi)}&\overline{a(U;x,-\xi)}\end{pmatrix}

(A.16)

is a matrix of symbols in
\left(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{m}\right)^{2\times 2}

and
{\bm{R}}(U)
is a real-to-real
matrix of smoothing operators in

\left(\widetilde{\cal R}_{p}^{-\varrho}\right)^{2\times 2}
.
Then, we may write

X(U)={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}_{1}(U;x,\xi)\right)}U+{\bm{R}}_{1}(U)U,

(A.17)

where the matrix of paradifferential operators
{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}_{1}(U;x,\xi)\right)}
is symmetric,
with matrix of symbols

{\bm{A}}_{1}(U;x,\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}a+a^{\vee}&b+\overline{b}\\
\overline{b}^{\vee}+b^{\vee}&\overline{a+a^{\vee}}\end{pmatrix}

(A.18)

and
{\bm{R}}_{1}(U)
is another real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in

\left(\widetilde{\cal R}_{p}^{-\varrho}\right)^{2\times 2}
.

The following is Lemma 3.21 in
[
27
]
.

Lemma A.15
.

Let
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varrho\geqslant 0
.
Let
{\bm{S}}(U)
be a matrix of spectrally localized homogeneous maps in
\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
which is linearly Hamiltonian of the form

{\bm{S}}(U)={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}(U;x,\xi)\right)}+{\bm{R}}(U)\,,

(A.19)

where
{\bm{A}}(U;x,\xi)
is a real-to-real
matrix of symbols in
\left(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}

as in (
A.16
),
and
{\bm{R}}(U)
is a real-to-real matrix of smoothing operators in
\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
.
Then,
we may write

{\bm{S}}(U)={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}_{1}(U;x,\xi)\right)}+{\bm{R}}_{1}(U),

where
the matrix of symbols
{\bm{A}}_{1}(U;x,\xi)
in
\left(\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}

has the form (
A.18
)
and
{\bm{R}}_{1}(U)
is another matrix of real-to-real smoothing operators in

\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
.
In particular the homogeneous operator

{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}{\OpBWPlain\left({\bm{A}}_{1}(U)\right)}
is linearly Hamiltonian.

A.3
Symplectic corrections

In this section we provide a symplectic correction to two different class of maps: linearly symplectic spectrally localized maps and smoothing perturbations of the identity.
The main result is Theorem 7.1 in
[
27
]
that we state below.

Theorem A.16
.

Let
p,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
p\leqslant N
,
K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with

K^{\prime}+1\leqslant K
,
r>0
.
Let

Z={\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
with
{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)\in\left({\mathcal{M}}^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2x2}
. Assume
that
Z(t)
solves a Hamiltonian system up to homogeneity
N
. Consider

\Phi(Z)\triangleq{\bm{B}}(Z;t)Z,

(A.20)

where

•

{\bm{B}}(Z;t)-{\rm Id}
is a matrix of spectrally localized maps in

{\bm{B}}(Z;t)-\textnormal{Id}\in\begin{cases}\left(\Sigma{\mathcal{S}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[r,N]\right)^{2x2},\qquad\qquad\text{if}\quad{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)={\rm Id}\,,\\
\left(\Sigma{\mathcal{S}}_{K,0,p}[\breve{r},N]\right)^{2x2}\,,\,\forall\breve{r}>0\quad\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(A.21)

•

{\bm{B}}(Z;t)
is linearly symplectic up to homogeneity
N
,
according to Definition
A.4
.

Then, there exists a real-to-real matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators
{\bm{R}}_{\leqslant N}(\cdot)
in
\left(\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\right)^{2x2}
,
for any
\varrho>0
, such that the non-linear map

Z_{+}\triangleq\big({\rm Id}+{\bm{R}}_{\leqslant N}(\Phi(Z))\big)\Phi(Z)

is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
and thus
Z_{+}

solves a system which is Hamiltonian up to homogeneity
N
.

Given a map of the form
U\mapsto U+{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H_{p+2}(U)
where
{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H_{p+2}(U)
is a
Hamiltonian, smoothing vector field, we find a correction which
is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
.

Lemma A.17
.

Let
p,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
p\leqslant N
.
Let
Y_{p+1}(U)={\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H_{p+2}(U)
be a homogeneous
Hamiltonian smoothing vector field in

\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{p+1}^{-\varrho}
for some
\varrho\geqslant 0
. Then there is a map of the form

\mathfrak{F}_{\leqslant N}(U)=U+Y_{p+1}(U)+F_{\geqslant(p+2)}(U),\qquad F_{\geqslant(p+2)}(U)\in\Sigma_{p+2}^{N}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho}_{q},

(A.22)

which is symplectic up to homogeneity
N
(Definition
A.11
).

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Lemmata 2.27 and 3.14 in
[
26
]
. A careful examination of the proofs reveals that
F_{\geqslant(p+2)}(U)
actually belongs to
\Sigma_{2p+1}^{N}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho}_{q}
. However, since this stronger result is not required for our purposes, we prefer to state the weaker conclusion
F_{\geqslant(p+2)}(U)\in\Sigma_{p+2}^{N}\widetilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{-\varrho}_{q}
.
∎

Remark A.18
.

The map
\mathfrak{F}_{\leqslant N}(U)
in (
A.22
) is indeed the truncation up to homogeneity
N
of the time-one flow generated by the Hamiltonian vector field
{\bm{J}}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H_{p+2}(U)
.

Appendix B
Auxiliary flows and conjugations

The following
conjugation Lemmata
B.1
and
B.2

are used in the nonlinear Hamiltonian Birkhoff normal form reduction performed in Section
7
. Their proof can be found in Appendix A of
[
27
]
.

The following hypothesis shall be assumed in both Lemmata
B.1
and
B.2
:

Assumption (A)
:
Assume
Z\triangleq{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)U
where
{\bf M}_{0}(U;t)\in\left({\mathcal{M}}^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
,
U\in B^{K}_{s_{0},{\mathbb{R}}}(I;\epsilon_{0})
for some
\epsilon_{0},s_{0}>0
and
0\leqslant K^{\prime}\leqslant K
.
Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and assume that
Z
solves the system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z=\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}\omega_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\xi)+{\rm i}a_{\leqslant N}(Z;\xi)+{\rm i}a_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)Z+\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}(Z)Z+\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)U,

(B.1)

where:

•

a_{\leqslant N}(Z;\xi)
is a real valued pluri-homogeneous
symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
;

•

a_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
is a non-homogeneous symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
with imaginary part

{\rm Im}\,a_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
in
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
;

•

\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}(Z)
is a real-to-real matrix of pluri-homogeneous smoothing operators in
\left(\Sigma_{1}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
;

•

\bm{R}_{>N}(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of
non-homogeneous smoothing operators
in
\left(\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

Lemma B.1

(
Conjugation under the flow of a Fourier multiplier).

Assume
(A)
at page
B
.
Let
g_{p}(Z;\xi)
be a
p
–homogeneous real symbol independent of
x
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{p}
,
p\geqslant 2
,
that we expand as

g_{p}(Z;\xi)=\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}}G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}\,,\qquad\overline{G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{-\vec{\sigma}_{p}}}(\xi)=G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)\in{\mathbb{C}}

(B.2)

and denote by
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}(Z)\triangleq\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}^{1}(Z)
the time
1
-flow defined in (
A.6
) generated by
\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}g_{p}(Z;\xi)\right)
.
If
Z(t)
solves system (
B.1
), then the variable

W\triangleq\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{G}}}_{g_{p}}(Z)Z

(B.3)

solves the system

\partial_{t}W={\rm i}\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)W+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}a_{\leqslant N}^{+}(W;\xi)+{\rm i}a_{>N}^{+}(U;t,\xi)\right)W+\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}^{+}(W)W+\bm{R}^{+}_{>N}(U;t)U,

(B.4)

where

•

a^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)
is a real valued pluri-homogeneous symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
, with components

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p-1}[a^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)]={\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p-1}[a_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)]\,,

(B.5)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{P}}_{p}\left[a^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)\right]={\mathcal{P}}_{p}\left[a_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)\right]+g^{+}_{p}(W;\xi),

where
g^{+}_{p}(W;\xi)\in\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{p}
is the real,
x
-independent symbol

g^{+}_{p}(W;\xi)\triangleq\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p},\vec{\sigma}_{p})\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}}{\rm i}\big(\vec{\sigma}_{p}\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\vec{\jmath}_{p})\big)G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}}(\xi)w_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p}};

(B.6)

•

a^{+}_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
is a non-homogeneous symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
with imaginary part
{\rm Im}\,a_{>N}^{+}(U;t,\xi)

belonging to
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
;

•

\bm{R}^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W)
is a real-to-real matrix of pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\left(\Sigma_{1}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho+c(N,p)}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
for some
c(N,p)>0
(depending only on
N,p
) and fulfilling

{\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p}[\bm{R}^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W)]={\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p}[\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}(W)]\,;

(B.7)

•

\bm{R}^{+}_{>N}(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\left(\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho+c(N,p)}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

The following lemma describes how a system is conjugated under
a smoothing perturbation of the identity.

Lemma B.2

(
Conjugation under a smoothing perturbation of the identity).

Assume
(A)
at page
B
.
Let
F_{\leqslant N}(Z)
be a real-to-real matrix of pluri-homogeneous smoothing operators
in
\left(\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{q}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}\right)^{2\times 2}
for some
\varrho^{\prime}\geqslant 0
.
If
Z(t)
solves (
B.1
)
then the variable

W\triangleq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leqslant N}(Z)\triangleq Z+F_{\leqslant N}(Z)Z

(B.8)

solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W={\rm i}

\displaystyle\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)W+\OpvecPlain\left({\rm i}a^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)+{\rm i}a^{+}_{>N}(U;t,\xi)\right)W+\bm{R}^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W)W+\bm{R}^{+}_{>N}(U;t)U,

(B.9)

where

•

a^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)
is a real valued pluri-homogeneous symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Sigma_{2}^{N}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{q}
, with components

{\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p+1}[a^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)]={\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p+1}[a_{\leqslant N}(W;\xi)]\,;

(B.10)

•

a^{+}_{>N}(U;t,\xi)
is a non-homogeneous symbol, independent of
x
, in
\Gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
with imaginary part

{\rm Im}\,a_{>N}^{+}(U;t,\xi)
belonging to
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
;

•

\bm{R}^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W)
is a real-to-real matrix of
pluri–homogeneous smoothing operators in

\left(\Sigma_{1}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho_{*}}_{q}\right)^{2\times 2}
,

\varrho_{*}\triangleq\min{(\varrho,\varrho^{\prime}-\frac{3}{2})}
(
\varrho\geq 0
is the smoothing order in Assumption
(A)
at page
B
), with components

{\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p-1}[\bm{R}^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W)]={\mathcal{P}}_{\leqslant p-1}[\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}(W)]\ ,

(B.11)

and, denoting
\bm{F}_{p}(W)\triangleq{\mathcal{P}}_{p}(\bm{F}_{\leqslant N}(W))
in
\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
, one has

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{p}[\bm{R}^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W)]={\mathcal{P}}_{p}[\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}(W)]

\displaystyle+d_{W}\big(\bm{F}_{p}(W)W\big)\left[i\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)\right]-i\bm{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(D)\bm{F}_{p}(W);

(B.12)

•

\bm{R}^{+}_{>N}(U;t)
is a real-to-real matrix of non–homogeneous smoothing operators in
\left(\mathcal{R}^{-\varrho_{*}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]\right)^{2\times 2}
.

In addition, if
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leqslant N}(Z)
in (
B.8
) is the symplectic up to homogeneity
N
map associated to a Hamiltonian vector field

\bm{G}_{p}(Z)Z=\bm{J}_{\mathbb{C}}\nabla H_{p+2}(Z)
as per
Lemma

A.17
, where
\left(\bm{G}_{p}(Z)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
has Fourier expansion

\big(\bm{G}_{p}(Z)Z\big)^{\sigma}_{k}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}z_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}\,,

(B.13)

then (
B.12
) reduces to

{\mathcal{P}}_{p}[\bm{R}^{+}_{\leqslant N}(W)]={\mathcal{P}}_{p}[\bm{R}_{\leqslant N}(W)]+\bm{G}_{p}^{+}(W),

(B.14)

where
\bm{G}_{p}^{+}(W)\in\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\varrho^{\prime}+\frac{3}{2}}_{p}\right)^{2\times 2}
is the smoothing operator with Fourier expansion

\displaystyle(\bm{G}_{p}^{+}(W)W)^{\sigma}_{k}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k,\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},-\sigma)\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{p+2}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm i}\big(\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}\cdot\vec{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(\vec{\jmath}_{p+1})-\sigma{\omega}_{\gamma,{\mathtt{b}}}(k)\big)G_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1},k}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1},\sigma}\,w_{\vec{\jmath}_{p+1}}^{\vec{\sigma}_{p+1}}\,.

(B.15)
